Book One

The Peanut Butter Underground

In the world after the next war, the Fatherhood commands everything
known. These Father’s of Society formed and took control of a
technocracy mutated from the decline, failure, and destruction of the
United States of America. Wars have worn the people haggard, afraid, and
cowed by the technology meant to empower.

Lawful means eroded the gossamer of our security when bartered for our
liberty. Dissent meets swift and harsh punishment. Technology now
shepherds mankind.

Secret police, the BLOXES, omniscient overhead, listening to every
breath. These Thinking-Drones trained on decades of smartphone, dash and
webcamera’s data when unleashed left the humans with little choice but
to comply. Compliance is Lawful.

Unlawful are sent to re-education centers to be beaten, broken, and
reformed; if they live past the beatings. Reform or death; there is no
other exit than death for the non-conformers. Cultural Perfection
tailored by the Fatherhood’s Directives, Edicts, and Executive Orders.
These father’s of our society are the idealists and calculators, the
Logicians and Chaos Calcutists, and Pontificators of theory into law and
law into a stilted socialize-credit score. In truth, this socialized
dogma drove the technocracy forward on the backs of the people in fear
of the Bloxes, programmed by the powermad elite. Fed by biometrics
recorded in real-time by the Bloxes overhead; Sempre Supra. Always
Above.

Noncompliance is Unlawful.

All things serve the people. The people serve the Fatherhood.

All things serve the Fatherhood.

Until one day. Deprecated and broken the life thought of as ideal and
perfected grinds mankind to dust. Breaking the hearts first, minds, and
bodies soon thereafter into the limping working class. Residents find
themselves burdened with work requirements for rations and shelter; the
Bloxes brutally delivered otherwise. Stratifications of this society
appear. Fatherhood elite always above all. Families of the elite fleshed
out the remaining body politic supposedly driving this world forward. As
if, they commanded atlas to shrug with mere words on paper. Working
class formed out of the same people they were formed of 25 or even 150
years ago, the people wanting freedom, fair equal treatment, and the
right to the fruits of their labor. The Fatherhood has sold long ago
that solidarity would only drive living conditions down. As the elite
force living conditions down with ever excessive financial burden,
schemes, and games driving the populace into a terrifying mental trap in
constant fear of their lives near the edge of collapse.

Soon, not too long from now the straw meant to break the people found
solidarity despite the generational-grievance fed faux-class war.

Will the people escape the techno-Authoritarian regime?

What will be left of humanity after the crush of the Fatherhood’s boot?

This is the Peanut Butter Underground.

Dystopian Afternoon

A few days into the month once known as March. The winter winds galed
and gusted through the concrete, glass, and steeled streets of the
capital.

Once a capital.

A capital always.

A war between then and now. A brutal uncivil war. The Northern War. The
Wars for South America followed. The state spans from the frozen
Greenland to the extent of the curl of Terra Del Fuego.

Wars drained the people forming this tortured culture. Whole chunks of a
thriving industrial base, now nearly all of them lay hallowed empty;
cold. The country once known as a land of promise and prosperity, now
lay in ruins.

Skeletons and crumbled mongrels found in Old York. Those tall buildings
now cracked half fallen. Once the palaces in the sky of the percent of
the percent. Now, long empty. Some never set foot in.

Now, the tallest of them stood broken, slipped in half, a jagged crack
exposing the center mass now more broken pencil than billionaire’s
enclave. The crush of the top now pullverized below the streets
impassable with destruction.

Even worse decay in a place known for brotherly love. Old townhomes long
before the American experiment now nothing but husks of prior form. The
massive arching terra cotta and provincial buildings; long admired for
the stout marble walls, seem to withstand even the flood sent by god
himself. All long blasted to atoms by the splitting of an atom.
Philadelphia was long uninhabitable as the fallout has also tainted much
of the place known once as Delaware and New Jersey.

Further south, inland of the Chesapeake Bay, on a patch of land flanked
by the Anacostia and Potomac remains the last ruins of a great
experiment.

This day, the winds blew mightily and whipped the loose collar of
tattered brown-green coat. The wind always follows the storms. High
speed derecho’s straight line winds through the canyons between concrete
buildings. Winds whipped into vortex’s and funnels whole tornados of
dust and debris. Many, some 13 stories high, buildings once full with
glass and steel facades, now stood empty whistling against the wind.

Persisting against the wind, we find a man. Tony stamped his feet and
slapped his thighs and arms trying to keep warm. He’s slipped into an
enclave providing relief from the cutting wind’s relentless march across
the city.

The rains and snow might be over for the year, there is always a faint
between winter and spring here in the capital city. Tony’s mind. -
desperate to think of anything but the cold - thought fondly into the
summer, not the last one, but one a long time ago before his first war.
Then terror attacks pepered the map broadcast by the news. And the fall
and winter came, a warm winter - nearly a double fall signaled a shift
in the climate.

Mankind ignored as much of the truth as it felt it needed to and the
wildfires began. losing whole cities in a matter of days on th west
coast. The east coasts faced incredible storms, hurricanes, and
tornados. Most o fit eastern seaboard left largely uninhabited apart
from a few portions of the major cities still standing - half in ruin.

Whole blocks were razed to the ground across the brownstones and
townhouses of the old foggy bottom of a city fondly once known as D.C.
Most of the city was now mere bones of old buildings. Georgetown,
largely empty, was a refuge for derelicts and smugglers using the
Potomac for their illicit highway. The Waterfront and Navy Yard
composing the south west and south east quadrants were now the main,
living city with Fatherhood’s Mall flanking it’s north. At the Potomac
end of the Fatherhood’s Mall a fallen and crumbled marble edifice to a
sitting man long blown apart by this country’s many civil wars. The
opposite end, reaching up to the apex of a hill lay the Capitol.
Fatherhood offices flanking each side of Fatherhood’s Mall from Great
Father Washington’s Monument.

The weather has sped the deterioration of the buildings. Any not
maintained and ordered maintained by the fatherhood have long since begu
their decay across the Northern quadrants of the old city. With nothing
and none for maintenance, many blocks atrophy in disuse.

It was in these blocks and buildings where Tony works. Reclaiming
fixtures and materials form old buildings and power lines for reuse and
repurpose. Some ancient places are filled with old rotting wood and
desolate. others opulent untouched palaces with such luxury it made his
eyes curse the wind and wet themselves.

Where the materials and coils of copper wire went, it was never evident,
it was never to the laborhood in the south west quadrant just bordering
the Potomac side of the Waterfront.

Much of the buildings are old federal buildings from the time before
Tony’s wars. A bitterness cast over the old gray and brown of a half
dead city. The famines took much of the population. The wars since,
during, and after have chained the remainder of their blood and
treasure. Many of the able-bodied are not. Tony’s scars trailed over his
right knee and thigh.

Unzipping the scar tracing from inside his right knee it cut sharply
across the kneecap then shot directly up his thigh over his quadriceps.
Under all that blood, muscle, and tissue we find a metal knee, knee cap,
and femur; it was the first thing saw when he woke up from the last
thing he remembered about his last war.

Tony’s first war found him hollow and empty. He spent parts of himself
in the desert he never thought could find again. His second war begged
to forget the first; his final war left him limping and no longer useful
as a soldier of the Empire.

Since, he’s followed the days and nights into years of drought chased by
deluge. The dry bitter winters cracked bridges nearly in half, and
months later, the same structures baked under brutal summer sun. Ferries
and boats replaced their use.

The war rages in the far distance on some other land, but Tony watches
the dividends trickle back to this place - men like him wearing old army
coats peppered the rest of the residents. Much of the toll of the wall
burdened shoulders both like his and not, but all in the working class
carried.

Here in the desolate city square, a newspaper whipped and weaved across
the plaza. A whistling from the edge of the building hung after the
rudeness of the pushy air. A man stood in an enclave shielded from the
whip of the wind. He clutched his green-brown coat close across his
chest with one hand, Then the less cold hand would spell the frozen one
and likewise.

His hands are dark skinned but grey and ashen from cold, but more so
scarred from the years of labor by them. He wore a hand knitted stocking
cap. A similar scarf of the same knit was tucked like a collar just
under the green-brown long coat - years past a new coat, but there
appeared many years of use between creation and now. The very edges,
nearly threadbare now and barely catching a wind while whipping at his
shins. The green-brown and “woolen” a synthetic fiber, made for and by
the Greatness campaigns of the war. Which war meant nothing. It was the
same souls that fought each of them.

Just as the bullets and gold and oil fought for a century ago were over
the same blood-soaked ground as today. But for his time served and the
time in the camps, Tony would be on a line drawn by a general fighting
an enemy he’d never seen, smelt, or sodden himself.

Stamping his feet and shifting side to side he attempted to keep himself
warm. He wasn’t where he should have been. He was supposed to be at the
trolley stop. A short ride and two blocks - home. But that was not the
case this evening. The trolley rolled away from the trolley stop. The
trolley was the last one. He'd have to find his own way by his feet
tonight. If this guy would show up with the stuff, he’d have been on a
trolley and rolling back to his side of town.

“Damn,” Tony cursed the wind, slapping his face. “Willie. Where the fuck
you be?”

The Raid

The explosion ripped the thin wooden door into the room. Raining shards
of wood, drywall, and dust on the occupants and shattering the silence
of the dimly-lit room and sending fragments of dust and a faux-brass
doorknob ricocheting over the heads of the dazed occupants.

A scream shot from a woman’s throat. Spoons clattered on the concrete
floor. Floating Black Boxes entered the room and hovered over the heads
of the occupants now cowering near the corners of the room. Green lights
illuminated dirty faces gasping from the violent intrusion. One brave,
but stupid, man swung a fragment of furniture at one box and was met in
the face with a blast of red sticky-spray that choked his nasal cavities
and induced a chemical reaction similar to a thousand maggots eating
them.

“This is the Blox!” A digitized voice shouted from the hallway. “Stop
Resisting! You are in violation of Judgment 34J-426!”

The Blox droned on while one by one the persons in the room dropped as
the Blox hovered above and sprayed liberally with the noxious red
liquid. Now unconscious and with their respiratory systems burning they
had no way of resisting.

“Wait! Wait!” One man shouted, shoving the open jar of brown goop in his
hands at a woman cowering next to him. “Don’t shoot! I give up! I’m not
with these people!”

A Blox noticed his hands stretching to the sky and released a thick
spurt of the red liquid, covering his face.

“Take them to the re-education center,” a Blox demanded men dressed in
gray coveralls stood outside of the room.

“That one there,” a Blox shined a blue ultraviolet light on a quivering
face, turning it an unnatural shade of lemon yellow, “He was the leader.
Take him to the Judges. They will decide his punishment.”

The Pedwalls

Tony shook his disappointment off with the last swig of numb juice and
tossed the bottle at a dumpster as he weaved a path across the street
grid. Time was not on his side. He had waited too long for the guy named
Willie. He was a “friend” of a guy that had better pain relief stuff
called “butter.” The numbjuice helped with the tiredness and soreness.
The banging ach of the metal knee and hip was constant, but the distance
easy enough to cover if he stayed to back alleys and utility
side-street. The booze warmed his chest and helped make the effort
slightly easier - at the moment. The hangover still would be on its way.
Numbjuice was welcomed after the day of breaking knuckles on salvage
assignments; and the only authorized drug for the labor class.

Curfew sirens ripped through the cold night air.

Shit! They are early! Or fuck, I’m later than I thought.

Five more blocks before the Pedwalls began to rise.

Pedwalls were tall steel and concrete dividers embedded by Fatherhood
orders to bisect intersections and blocked whole “protected” blocks.
Above between the buildings bucketlines spring to action, commercing
between buildings despite the Pedwalls disruption below. But there was
no getting a person between buildings.

Bloxes partolled all night. anything larger than a cat was usually
sprayed unconscious and dragged into a magistrate chamber. Then, only
they and the magistrates could tell you the outcome. When the wind was
too strong to fly, the Bloxes adhered to the sides of walls and
recharged off power-taps they vampired from power outlets struck into
and on the side of buildings and sidewalks wherever a power coupling was
needed. There on these perches Bloxes never blinked and always watched.
As Tony ran, Bloxes like black octagons peered out red scan lines taking
survey of the landscape for their evening vigil. Tony ran through one
line and a Bloxe barked but could do nothing as the wind destabilized
drone flight. but the Blox had noted the biometric signature and
associated infractions. Not now, but later the Bloxes never forgot and
networked their memories.

Still enough time, Tony could find his way closer to his block and avoid
a night sleeping under flattened boxes or maybe he’d have to hide in a
sewer. That or get rounded up by the Blox.

Cutting down an alleyway Tony caught the tailwind of luck and saw a
clearer path to an intersection just a block from his.

The low rumble of the Pedwalls vibrated under his feet at a full sprint
to make the last 10 meters.

Blinding heat sizzled in his legs and lungs as his muscles struggled to
flex and deconstruct - running toward the rising pedwalls.

Slowly. the three foot materker flashed yellow rising from underground.

He was almost there and with another gallop full of luck.

A high jump it wasn’t. A long jump, it should not have been. The blood
flow and envirgoration triggered a synaptic delirium, Tony thought he
could hurdle the rising six foot steel wall in his path.

Instead, his ill-timed jump laid himself across the top of the Pedwall.
With a leg almost thrown over, crushing his groin and abdomen along the
rising edge.

The Pedwall did not slow, rising now a little over seven feet and then
as the rest of his momentum slammed into then carried his over the rest
of the wall, topsy turvy.

After the world landed around him, he lay crashed on the other side of
the erect pedwall. Heaped and hurting, Tony found himself breathing in
dust form the pavement as he slowly shifted arms and joints to test for
breakage.

Like a scarecrow fallen from his perch, Tony slowly unwound his body and
slowly tested his extremities. Around him the block was nearly empty.
But for one - Illisa. The name of the woman standing cross-armed and
hip-shifted watching her husband collect himself from crashing into and
over the Pedwall.

He rubbed a shoulder as his lower back pinched a ligament near his right
hip. Tony began limping toward the apartment building’s entry.

Illisa scowled.

“Honey! Sweetheart! You’ll never believe…” Tony started to explain,
“what happened… at work… today.” Illisa had already left her perch
slamming the door with her exit.

Tony ceased his explanations. His shoulders sank. Then his lower back
reminded him of his inherent ability to prove exponentially the depth of
his stupidity.

At least he didn’t have to be sober for this. He thought, the Numbjuice!

He patted his coat pocket for the bottle only to find a damp overcoat
with broken glass now cutting through the threadbare layers.

For a moment, he debated facing the Blox over Illisa.

Letting out a sigh, Tony heaved himself forward to popping knees
cracking like fireworks from inside. He grimaced, but proceeded forward
regardless of the scar tissue left from his first war. The arthritis and
daily ache was courtesy of a life tolled in labor. The constant ring in
his ears from all of Tony’s wars.

Due Process in Dystopia

The dank courtroom lay in the belly of the courthouse intake and
diversionary process center. One side a courtroom like any you would
expect. Benches, counselors tables, a bench for the judge and a desk for
a clerk. But the other half was nothing more than a loading dock where
an overhead door rolled up announcing the busses arrival.

A long tall school bus painted green and grey with high-visibility tape
placarding Enforcement Division along its side backed into a loading
dock.

Yellow lights emitted a slight hum overhead. Thumping and the whine of a
self-inking stamp spun the pad to press and back again, with the papers
flying underneath. A hunched-backed clerk busied himself at a wooden
desk just under the Judge’s bench a few feet above. The rush of diesel
exhaust flooded the courtroom, while the clerk grimaced and pulled a
facemask up over his nose.

The desk was not his own but was rather the setting for the position
that the judge ordered him to occupy. And there he sat stamping papers
until his honor deemed necessary for the clerk to perform another task.

Suddenly bursting through the bus’s double back doors and into the
courtroom, two grey coverall clad men dragged a captured man toward the
clerk and dropped him flat on his face to the yellow tiled floor. The
lump of flesh moaned as the submission fluid began to wear off.

“Don’t just drop that anywhere, you piggy flubs!” the clerk shouted.
“Pick him up.”

The gray men did as ordered and gripped the moaning body by the
shoulders and lifted from the ground barely a groan from any of them.

“Now, you can drop him there,” the clerk smugly ordered.

Again, the body hit the hard tile floor with a thump and groan.

“Where is the paperwork?” the clerk stated without looking up from a
fresh stack of unstamped but yellowing paperwork.

One of the movers offered a folded piece of paper towards the clerk. He
snatched it away from the movers hand. Unfolding the paper with the tips
of his fingers. Smoothing it out in front of him, the clerk rolled his
eyes over the document, not for content, but rather completion. An
impatient sigh hissed from the clerk.

“There is no signature on line twenty-four. There must be at least one
signature online twenty-four!” He flourished the clutch of papers at the
men.

They remained inert as the gas expelled from their open mouths.

“Here!” He thrust the paper back to the mover that gave it to him and
said. “Your mark will have to do.”

A pen quickly followed the paper.

Awkwardly griping the pen; the Mover marked a crooked sprawling lines
over the space directly above the line to the right of number
twenty-four. The cerci snapped th pea-per back leaving a long line from
th mover’s last scratch.

Back to his stamping, the clerk ignored the three of them.

Thump…thump thump…thump thump thump…thump thump.

Without breaking his rhythmic pattern of stamping - the clerk slapped th
papers on the edge of the desk and the mover grabbed them, gripping and
crinkling them in his passive paw.

“William Menendez!” The clerk shouted over the thumping of the stamping
on the paper. “What is your plea?”

The hump of humanity laid flat on his face where the movers had dropped
him. It did not utter a sound.

“William Menendez!” The clerk shouted again.

A groan emanated from the underside of a pink face.

“What…is…your…plea?” Now annoyed, the clerk huffed at the unresponsive.

Another groan puffed from a puffy face as the mass of flesh and bone
rolled over onto its back.

“Of all the insubordinate…ill programmed…stuttering flubbers. Of all the
luck! On such a lovely night as tonight.” The clerk continued to grumble
as Willie slowly regained consciousness. The clerk leaned forward and
looked over the edge of the archaic wooden desk and asked,
“How…do…you…plead?”

“Who are you?” Willie responded. “Where am I?”

“What insolence! Arrogant Flub!” The clerk thought as he furiously began
stamping papers.

Willie, still dazed from the submission fluid, rubbed his eyes and
sensed the chemical aftertaste in his mouth and nose. A paper descended
down from the clerk’s desk and settled like a feather on Willie's chest.

“Three weeks of reprogramming! One-week solitary! Guards!” the clerk
shouted and two green suited men appeared from a doorway across the
room, “Hereby Renamed Criminal 6841, formerly Willie Menendez, to
Reprogramming!”

Stamp thumps slapped the papers in triplicate and replaced customary
gavel strikes.

“Huh? Who what now?” Willie puzzled, “What in the mother-grabbing...”

“Four weeks!” The clerk spit words at Willie as the Guards - the Movers,
the papers had “promoted” them now to Guards - dragged him across the
room.

“Four?!”

“Five and Solitary!”

The door slammed and ended Willies crash course in dystopian judicial
procedure.

A Good Dystopian Morning

We begin with liberty’s orphans amongst a revolution of failures. Once a
great city - the capital of a nation. One that carries a name long
tarnished. An empire now a husk of itself in its twilight days.
Centuries before men rejected the chains of a king. This experiment,
once called a republic, carried a great success for hundreds of years.
The ideal carried with its itizens and all the future generations of the
ones cast out from one to another country and quickly added to the
melting pot.

Generations of blood fed this republic from the flood of the
battlefields in a bitter brotherly war to the republic turned empire.
The promise of democracy’s prowess only loosened the grip of liberty
bartered for security. It was not for tens of generations before the
people understood they had never lost anything; realizing their lives
were no longer theirs.

At seven o’clock every morning sirens tore the peace of the early day.
Birds shot through the air. The sun cracked the horizon. The city found
itself bathed in a cold light. The Pedwalls rose at midnight and lowered
at seven everyday as summoned by the cry of the siren. Eight-foot tall
concrete walls, each one foot thick at every major intersection, slowly
lowered into the pavement offering a low moaning grind of their weight
to roil the thin soles of the worker’s boots.

At fifteen minutes after eight o’clock a clutch of musicians gathered
near an intersection. For six years these few chosen musicians donned
their marching uniforms and brass instruments. They were the last of any
of the rest of the residents who even knew how to play those
damn-fangled contraptions.

Twenty minutes after eight o’clock rousing patriotic music roared
through the concrete jungle. Rousing and playing brightly they played
tarnished brass as they bayed and moo’ed a frenetic spray of patriotic
rhythm and rouse. The melody was something resembling a national anthem.
Perhaps one once heard at the stadium in the abandoned part of the city.

The workers trickled form dwellings toward tramlines and soon much of
the housing dwells were largely empty and would remain for sometime, at
least until the day's labor was to be called, “done.”

“WASH..TON. NAT.ON..S.”

After the labor of another day was done. Tony rode aboard the tram that
took him to the homeblock everyday. The city’s bones lay before his
gaze.

The dull yellow light inside the tram cast an intermittent reflection
against the glass a few handwidths from Tony’s face. His reflection
greeted him with a bushy mass of woolen hair. The next flash revealed
the rough patchy outline of a barely kept beard.

“I need a pair of shears.” His thoughts wandered. The Tram shifted and
bumped to another line and turned on a cross street toward a structure
curving behind the streets as the tram passed. The massive structure
grew with each block forming itself and imposing its walled outside
against the old crumbling brownstones and caved-in warehouses.

A half fallen billboard bore scraps of an advertisement meant for a time
long before Tony’s wars. BRIGHTENED AND REFRESHED.

“Clumsy.” Tony’s thoughts rolled as the bend of the stadium took shape
while he rode.

A tall arching gate came into view, turnstiles and gates along with
heavy ironed fencing pulled next to the tram.

“STADIUM, EAST WATERFRONT!” The driver hollered over his shoulder as if
they were mere cattle. “MANDATED BREAK!”

The driver drove his right leg down into the horizontal pedal under the
dash as the tram lurched low jerking to a grinding halt. Slapping the
brake engaged and tapping the electric engine off, the driver flamed a
cigarette and alighted to the curb before his passengers had recovered
from the halt.

Fifteen minutes; timed and strictly adhered to by the Trammers Union.

In truth, the Fatherhood pitted all clases against one and another; even
the Merchant against the Administration Classes; just as the Trammers
against other Laborers. The drivers enjoyed a monopoly on labor within
the guise of a bargaining position at a table they believed was equal to
that of the Fatherhood.

Tony pulled himself up out of his wooden seat and toward the exit to
stretch his legs and back.

Reaching inside his window, the driver retrieved a bottle and took a
long swish. After a gulp, the driver spat a wash of water from his mouth
and lit another cigarette as he eyed Tony watching him return his water
bottle to his seat.

Exiting the Tram, Tony shook off the driver’s glare and looked back at
the Half sunk marquee for the stadium.

Most Trammers were earnest, honest people. They work. They earn. They go
home. They live. But even a frugal Trammer still rode a Tram. Only the
Fatherhood rode in the cars and movers. Members were the only class that
could ever afford the privilege of something taking one person from one
place to another - at leisure; inconceivable to those who rode a Tram.

Tony wondered to himself, sat and wondered, about this section, or
neighborhood as it was once known, before Tony’s wars. The structure
took the form of a badly built massive bowl with a wide open side that
would not hold any water past the first ring of seating and stands
encircling the low patched overgrown field.

Debris, wreckage, and trash littered the once green space, Tony’s eyes
grew tired and watery as he felt the cold edge of the fence line
separating him from the stadium.

Wondering to himself, “Why were all those seats set to stare back into
itself, as if the whole structure was meant to contemplate its navel. A
church perhaps. I’m likely very wrong.”

Time wandered around the scene wasting itself while twilight began
eating the sky.

DEPARTING! The driver hollered at the passengers crowding back aboard as
Tony joined the push. Now, inside and back into his seat, already an
ache grew in his metal knee and burred slowly up his leg.

The building towered over the tram as Tony looked out from inside
catching a reflection of the old half-missing marquee.

It read, “WASH..TON. NAT.ON..S.”

Tomorrow in Dystopia

At twenty-two minutes after eight o’clock, Tony remained in his bed. A
thin pillow twice-folded under his head. A sheet dragged across his hips
and rested along a line of his hip and abdominal wall, which were also
nicely defined - but not for attractive means, more out of labor and
wars and many times over since then. The toil of his life has borne a
toll on this man. His crooked spine in childhood nearly kept him from
the fervor of the first war he remembered. It did not prohibit him from
conscription into the next. The early morning sunlight floods from thin
horizontal windows running along the outside wall of the bedroom.

Tony rememained. Half covered. One arm tucked behind his head pinned
between a folded pillow. The other tracing a string of scars from his
last day as a soldier.

His mind trailed back toward a time before the scars pock-marked his
abdomen and ribcage curling toward his shoulder blades. The helicopter
crash nearly cut Tony in half. His life was nearly gone when the
Airborne Geolocating Life Support unit (AGLS) plucked him from the
wreckage, stabilized his wounds, then rocketed him toward more intensive
medical services. He could not recall directly but Tony swore he
remembered floating in the bio-tank while the AGLS streaked along the
desert floor back toward the firebase for intensive medical treatment.
He swore he heard his Drill Sargent’s voice garbled in the bubbles of
the biogel. He heard the shout of his father’s voice echoing in his
mind’s ear.

He had been awake for the past hour and twenty-two minutes staring at
the three-foot long crack in the ceiling just above his head. For that
same amount of time he had been wondering about the events of that day.
Where those scars came from. How the shrapnel curved from the birth of
the explosion to find its target on his side - and now also filling the
same scar in his mind’s eye. He remembered the help of the rifle hanging
in his hands and dragging from the sling across his shoulders. The heat
of the day bore down its unrelenting heat. Layers of issued equipment
laid soaked and laminated to his underside and all the normal sweating
places of a man. Tony’s mind eye flashed back tot he officers arguing
and screeching into the command channels so loudly he torn the earpiece
out and left it dangling.

A moment passed. his vision trailed off toward the valley floor below
them. Patched and green grasses traced a trickling stream at its lowest
point, then orange and brown layers on each other leading to a rocky and
looser layer - the ridge over the valley below. Tony remembered looking
down an dstmasping his foot on the rocky dusty land underfoot. Then he
didn’t rmember much after the second foot stamp.

A small brown radio with a single three-inch speaker buzzed alive and
piercing with static gave way to a calm and resolute announcer
sputtering and mumbling a news broadcast. His drone outlined the
flowering praise heaped on itself. Self-glory was a cornerstone of the
Fatherhood’s grip on this world. Members demanded swift vengeance if the
trinity of a member is offended - Image, vanity, or pride. Bloody
vengeance doled along the twists and turns of the palace intrigue.

His mind distant and uninterested, Tony grasped the paper up, already
soaking through and he had barely sipped a quarter the way. The news
droned on the danger of street drugs peddled by subversives.

Twenty people were arrested and three hundred pounds of narcotics were
confiscated. The radio was always on. Either lightly playing the
propaganda marches or delivering news. Always on; and listening to.

Other than the streak of brown desert sand that connected to the horizon
from inside a half-tracked scout vehicle, Tony had never seen foreign
soil since his last war and not likely to ever again. Forget it, he
would never dare.

The chronic strength-sapping pain pinched and ground his metal knee
attached by synthetic tensile-tendon to his tactical-aluminium femur.

If the drain wasn’t centered in his knee and hip, it centered along his
lower spine and gripped a string of lumbar-thoracic vertebrae.

“Get up already! They’ll come for you if you’re late again!” Illisa’s
voice shouted from the other room. “Tony! Tony, wake up! You lazy flub,
wake up!”

Tony heard stamping footsteps growing louder. His back hurt more today,
than his knee. The footsteps stopped, Tony’s eyes had closed.

She stared down at him, as Tony’s eyes refocused in the light the crack
in the ceiling now extended from top of her head like a thin broken horn
curling from her left ear.

“I’m up,” Tony said. “I’ll be up and gone in a few minutes.”

She huffed and stomped away back to the kitchen.

He slowly sat up and rubbed at the spots where it always hurt. He eyed
two knee braces hooked over the back of a chair just in front of him.

The black metal of the braces had grown rusty over the years, and red
build-up could be seen at its hinges. The braces had been issued to him
after the last war he fought.

The black skin of his knees stretched over scars trailing along his
inner leg. He raised his leg and with his free hand rubbed at the sore
muscle and tendons under the taught dry skin. The dots lined like a
zipper from the surgery that removed him from the last war he fought in.

Before the end of Tony’s last war, a bullet shattered his kneecap and
upper femur; now under the scar traced by his fingers lay a metallic
alloy. Reaching behind the knee and pressing his thumb at the base of
his calf muscle.

It ached in the cold. It ached in the heat. It was felt and nearly
always it was a negative to Tony’s senses. Stronger than the rest of his
bones and would last longer than his organic parts by centuries, Tony
couldn’t remember the time before his wounds. As if the balsts that
twisted and scarred his body were paying an insurance policy.

Sitting up and throwing his legs off the bedside, he pulled a crumpled
shirt from the floor and threw it over his wide shoulders. He scratched
at his thick, black, woolen hair gathered along his jaw line and wished
for a sharp razor to clean the matted mess away, razors – sharp ones -
hadn’t been available in the rations for quite some time.

He strapped the steel braces to his knees and sighed as his day
officially began. A loud pop sound from his right knee as he stood up.
There was a quick snap of pain but then a warm dull sensation followed
it. He could never say for sure, but he swears he could feel the grind
of the metal joint. A pressure grew in his crotch.

Now living together for three years, Illisa and Tony had been paired by
winning a lottery. A lottery that rewarded the “winner” with a stranger
they were now paired to a mate and “expected to make more of oneself.”

The lottery curbed a shortage in housing and with hopes to increase a
stagnating birth rate. However, it did little for either issue in the
course of implementation.

Issued a number to every single-adult; pink and blue tickets. Bloxes
overhead. A mass of humanity gathered in the city square and ordered to
mingle until they found their matching number. Benefits were allowed to
those who went home together that night – and extra bottles of numbjuice
and real bars of chocolate. The numbjuice was a luxury three times more
intoxicating than regular alcohol but still quite sweet to the tongue.

But for Tony, no amount of numbjuice could dull the reality that his
matching number drank his ration of numbjuice every week.

Illisa generally ignored Tony’s concerns that tend to arise from the
situation of two people living together.

Once Tony found a man’s shirt a size too small for him under the bed.

“How should I know where it came from? Why should I keep up with your
things? You don’t keep up with mine!” Illisa’s responses sounded
rehearsed even now when it was heard in Tony’s mind.

Since this memory, Tony had found three shirts, a pair of boots that
were a size too small to be his own, and at times an extra ration of
numbjuice in the cabinet. He stopped asking about the new things in
their sparse dwelling.

After a long piss in the bathroom. He slipped his tattered sock covered
feet into a pair of workboots. Pulling the coveralls over his shoulders
and zipping it to mid-chest, his knee braces squeaked as he moved toward
a long brown-green coat hanging on a hook by the dwelling exit. Now in
his boots he was through the living space and out of the dwelling with a
few strides. Leaving without saying a word to Illisa.

Before and After Pacification Night

Tony pulled the door closed and smoothed the collar of his tattered coat
and began walking down the narrow hallway towards the lift.

A man stumbled past him in the hallway of the dwelling-complex, the man
smelt thick with the scent of numbjuice and nicotine.

Tony turned a corner of the hallway, and heard the faint sound of
Illisa’s voice welcoming someone into their dwelling. He begged his
anxiety to cease imagining his cuckolding.

It was not paranoia; it was in fact, true - Illisa had a low level
Administrator she milked for rations, perks, and “privileges.”

Bile rose in his throat, but if he turned around he would have been late
for his reclaiming work and may find himself sprayed by a Blox and a
week in the sweatbox for his pride.

Stairs curved around the broken lift as Tony’s knees braces creaked
further down from the sixth floor.

The smell of trash and rotting food permeated his nose as he stepped out
into the early morning sun. Humanity’s detritus - yesterday’s rotting
food, alongside today’s ration delivery. Dropped by delivery drones in
the early hours of the day they dispersed like bear-sized starlings out
of the distribution centers and splayed across the inhabited places of
the old city.

Not all Bloxes were flat and square; basically flying rectangles. Some
bigger than others, but generally the same shape for similar utility -
controlling humans. Larger flying bear-sized Bloxes supported the
logistics of this city. Above Tony’s head fluttered one with a shaking
grasp on a delivery that had slipped and dropped mid-intersection before
disappearing around a corner.

The crashed ration box poured onto the cold wet street. A grip of
Bloxes - roughly five to six - descended to the scene setting up a
perimeter around the rations. Puddles and paper dotted the cracked
concrete as Tony’s boots carried him along a path that led him to the
tram stop.

Barking short honking bursts, two collection Bloxes - floating
dumpsters - descended from a nearby alleyway to the grounded bounty.
Clean up drones floated down and quickly scooped away debris. When the
last of anything of value was callously thrown away into the dumpster
drones, the security drones lifted off and floated back out of sight,
but always watching.

Between two rivers the city once spread across both of them to cover the
whole delta watershed. The spaces known south of the Anacostia - called
AnaSouth, spread across the riverside with an old park half reclaimed
and flooded by the bend of the river. A fractured bridge lay a broken
umbilical cord between the city and AnaSouth. Only ferries traversed
between; lawful ones during the day, unlawful at night.

The other places were west of the main city on the opposite side of the
Potomac lay an old cemetery, there was nothing left there. Desolate and
west of the city, none wanted to live with the dead.

The City remained in the center - AnnaSouth to the right, the
DesolateWest. Waterfronts flanking southwest. An industrial Navy Yard to
its southeast.

The city center was a fraction of its past expanse. A string of old
avenues and curving parkways left strange gardens of curving cracked
pavement now pitted and lined with weed and wildflowers. There is a tall
domed capital building. Flanked with the only buildings in the city that
are never empty. The Fatherhood commanded the world from this perch.

Spiralling out from the capital trams spidered into the old city. Nearby
the Waterfront and the last place where Bloxes and the Conforming Raids
do not bother. Prior to the Fatherhood’s reign this was a place for
entertainment and relaxation. Long ago, the leisure of this worlds past
left the crumbling hulks of stadiums and now worthless infrastructure.
Now, the Waterfront, sick and polluted with debris, trash, and the
rotting carcasses of boats. Now only smugglers dare the shallow waters
with their illicit wares.

Long before the Fatherhood. Long before the ones before them and
centuries prior to the founding fathers. This swamp land curved along a
river nearly six homeblocks wide. The waters fetid and awful with run
off. Man carved this capitol out of muck and mud. The imposition of
order on the chaos underfoot was the most daring expense in those simple
days.

The cornerstones laid within ceremony and the moment cherished gave way
to only a moments of acomplishment throw to the bonfire the production
and progress. The exhaustion of only work known for so many drove them
mad; rotten with complacency. Now those days were far removed to ancient
history far from the worries of the day.

Pacification Night the streets were “soothed and made nice.” Military
Personnel Carriers and school buses roamed the District that night.

Old roadblocks and shattered vehicles remained from the last
pacification. Charred concrete and uprooted trees showed the power fo
the Fatherhoods enforcers. Mounted to the turrent of tanks, the sonic
weapons deafened the protestors; the pulse cannons charred the ground
and wielded bolts of electricity tagged and paralyzing whole crowds of
residents. Many never wake up.

Once the shock drained from the scene, armored police invaded the scene
with two masses of troops pushing up the street with one supporting from
the rear, and circulating in fresh boots.

Their dark blue uniforms carried patches of the Enforcement Division.
Rings of zip-tie restraints lined their black belts balanced with a long
stun baton on each. Plexiglass shield laced with electric shock strips
pressed against protestors. The mass of troops crashed into jittering
and twitching people, barking at their helplessness.

“STOP RESISTING! NO RESISTANCE AUTHORIZED!”

From the window of the tram, the memory shook Tony’s very core. In a
moment, blood ran in gallons over the concrete under Tony's boots. His
mind lied that he was back in the war before his last. The popping of
electricity from less-than-lethal weapons followed with the screeching
of turbine engines overhead.

Sempre Supra, lined the drones and helicopters sides with blocked
numbers differentiating one form another floated and swooped above in
Tony’s memories now flashing back all too real for his present
condition.

His mind heard small arms rounds pinging the steel hide of the armored
transport.

His chest cut short, quick, sucking breaths.

The memory shot into Tony’s mind. Fire seared uniform to ash and burned
his skin from the kinetic round that had punched through the explosive
armor layer meant to deflect the deadly round. Instead the round punched
through and injected a spray of molten phosphorus into the belly of the
vehicle.

The liquid fire covered Tony’s driver - liquifying and melting
everything the man, the pilot chair, the controls now all dripping and
pooling liquid steel speckled with errant bits of what metal he wore -
buttons, buckles, etc. One image caught Tony’s memory and now he could
not forget it.

The driver was a soccer player. He played in college before his draf
into the war. He talked a good game and when they played pick up games
in basic he was pretty good. He always complained about his left foot.
He said he had a break in it when he was a kid and the doctors put a
plate in there. He always said it got sore in the cold winters. He
showed Tony a picture of the X-ray after the surgery. The little string
of hexagons lined up along the grayish shade of bone and the screws into
the bone itself left ragged in the contrast of the bone. Floating in the
molten soup where the driver once sat, eyelets from destroyed boots
emulsified into the lava with a string of three hexagons dissolving.

Time slows and speeds up in Tony’s memories. Some moments rushed by
incredibly fast and he lost any eye for details, but for this moment,
Tony’s mind could not be moved any faster than a painfully slow pace as
sparks and sprays of hell peppered Tony’s leg.

The navigator - seated along side Tony in the loader’s positon - caught
the brunt of the blast and lay half melted pooled and molten into his
station. The firing grip held tight by his left hand - a ring on the
third finger gripped along the rest of the hand to a wrist and then only
charred stubs of bone and flesh.

Tram brakes screeched and rattled the Tram to a stop. The shudder and
noise brought Tony out of the panic. He didn’t hear the stop name.
Rather he launched from his seat toward the door opening to his escape.

Feet on concrete. He tapped his shoulders and counted; counted, and
breathed just as the counselor taught him. The phosphorus burns took
chunks of his right leg leaving dime-sized pockmarks of smooth-scarred
divots. Still, years and miles removed, the nerve pain hot and raw holes
left in his senses.

Off the Tram he stomped into an unseen puddle sending a splash of filthy
water everywhere. As it lapped his boot, he saw the blood from that
night again.

Breaths sucked and cut through his teeth as Tony begged numbers out of
his chest attempting to use the calming techniques for his post
traumatic stress disorder; Tony’s final war gift.

The time on his wrist said the next tram would take more time than it
would be to walk at this point - he leaned toward his HomeBlock despite
the ache of his physical and mental wounds.

He passed by a charred hunk of concrete. The ashes of Pacification night
have long since been washed into the drains, sewers, and bowels of the
city, but the fires that night still smoldered below the surface.

After the cultural evolutions froze the city, many of the glass and
steel buildings that marked the skyline grew unstable for inhabitation.
The largest buildings were in danger of caving in - older citizens
mumbled quietly that the buildings were crumbling out of shame.

Since the cultural evolutions entire sections of the city remained cut
off. AnaSouth had long been left to rot across the river. On quiet clear
nights you could still see across the Potomac the gridded graves laid
like as blanket .

Brownouts and browndowns occur daily. Transmission lines sagged in the
distance. Solar panels atop the highest buildings are not producing like
the Fatherhood once believed they would - neglected by design. Steam
boilers underground pushed the Pedwalls; on time and like clockwork just
not efficiently enough to feed the city. Wind farms not visible from the
even the highest towers of the old city gined electrons through
underground cables.

Polutions had cast the city in a browned-orange hue. Granite masonry
walls supported the monoliths while cracked concrete underfoot chipped
and crunched away revealed broken cobblestones. Much of the city lay
disused, empty. Many buildings decreed uninhabitable after the bombs
fell from the war before Tony’s first war. Now, the only occupants and
tresspassers were the unlawful.

The riverside still teemed with life, at all hours - lawful and
unlawful. Some hours more favorable other less so. With the best work
done under darkness; always avoiding full moons as there was no shield
form the Blox sensors.

This trade grew. The components to their product sifted and refined form
the base elements of a few chemicals siphoned from the industrial
supplies.

Then a few days of refinement gifted the brave with a bounty of goop,
some preferred crystalline and dried versions, but there were no other
drugs than “PNaUt But2r.”

Phosphorus

Erbium

Arsenic

Nitrogen

Titanium

Boron

Ruthenium

Peanut Butter was poison. Uncut, it would kill an unaccustomed user.
Whole “jars” would satisfy tens of people at a time, many junkies found
groups happy to partake once a jar was obtained. The other type was made
of peanuts and before the droughts was enjoyed. Since then, synthetic
foods have been in ration drops - rarely fresh vegetables, mainly canned
and formed foods.

The drug however, was in abundance. A small scoop was just enough to set
a person into a warm distant place far from the awfulness of reality.

The waterfront and docks fed the drug factory, the factory fed the
streets. The Bloxes avoided and could not infiltrate the underground
distribution network. Pedwalls blocked the underground when lowered for
the day. When raised they opened up whole underground “streets” making
the distribution, sale, and consumption of a substance known as Peanut
Butter.

Only the brave attempted the underground. Fears of cannibals and demons
traded among the gossipers and mentally enslaved had evolved into
sincere beliefs that one would lose their soul if they dared go down.

Like the city above it, the system below came about by will of muscle
and blood, the city was built on generations of sweat, as the strong
hands of workers formed the graceful shapes of nature out of solid
bedrock.

The road and buildings still alive with utility were all products of
sweat. Sweat on the brow and face, which at one time was not a sign of
discomfort but rather a badge of honor. The Underground then took these
blueprints and slowly more and more underground lands sprinted under
homeblocks across the old city.

Above ground, this city was built in an era when, on holidays, grown men
would take their children to the building in which they had poured
foundations, or hung drywall, or laid waterpipes and told their sons,
“This is my building. I helped build it. With my own hands.”

Underground, the few that could find a moment to rest found themselves
exhausted from the brutal toil demanded by this future state of mankind,
often failing to cherish their time with their offspring. If they were
allowed to know their children at all.

Half of the city crumbled and was uninhabitable, the other half had been
subject to cultural-evolutions and social toiling of the Fatherhood.
Whole neghborhoods firebombed with its survivors ordered to survive
elsewhere and directed to another quadrant to find another mass of
confusion and disillusion.

Food pantries grew larger and larger. the lines for help curved through
blocks. The Food riots grew out of this desperation and reduced the
streets to caches of violence and eruptions of fear.

Bloxes followed. Their airborne frames began simple and utilitarian for
function and performance. They swooped out of the night sky on the first
night of pacification. After the people lay stunned and helpless. Movers
produced from alleyways loaded and heaved them into wagons and backs of
buses. Soon the busses and transports brimmed in lines stringing to the
courthouse intake center.

After that first night, the next night of pacification was a sword
forever hanging over the people’s neck.

With some much of the people taken away, new Bloxes surfaced and the
logistics of old boxy moving trucks were soon no longer needed in the
inner city.

Logistic Bloxes soon commanded right of way and with a whim beckoned the
Security Bloxes to guard against the inefficient and guard from “waste
and abuse.” They guarded the supplies and the health of the transport
system, and easily turned on the hungry people.

The food riots gave way to the gulags. Fed by the disappearances. And
then the rations became more regular; children less hungry - yet hungry
nonetheless.

A life chained is no life. The labor classes drunk on numbjuice rations
and barely clothed by their labors found themselves having less
children. Edict and proclamation demanded more children for the future
of the country’s, but deaf ears found little reason to listen for love.

Not from any horrible biological disease or from a war waged by a power
mad dictator against an evil enemy. Not from any obvious depletion of
human resources. People were disappearing. The city ached when the wind
cut through it. Winds came all the time now, with the lack of trees onf
all sides of the town. The barren dust fields outside the city gates
yielded an inedible harvest - trees spoiled at their taproots by the
wild thirst of the moneychangers long ago. Although the soy-stockpile
fed the city, remaining as empty as it was left nearly barren since last
season's drought.

Equality Has No Price; Id Faciendum Est

The Fatherhood directed everything. Every inch of the city was watched,
save the darkness of the underground; every breath sucked in and out of
one’s lungs was instructed. All companionships were arranged and
sanctioned by them. Every scrap of ration food was inspected and stamped
in triplicate. Paperwork abounded in the service centers. All this paper
work was commanded by the Fatherhood born out of antiquated traditions
of generations before.

Equality has no price; the slogan plastered on the sides of abandoned
buildings and emblazoned clearly at the top of the public decrees that
littered the ground and danced in the wind gusts.

Id faciendum est; This must be done. This is the current
cultural-evolution slogan and announced just three weeks ago.

Persons wanting anything needed permission and sanction to do it in this
over-legislated city. Lottery winners were allowed children, but many
were not allowed to keep them. Dwellings were analogously divided to the
inhabitants; all people were placed in dwellings along side of other
people who both resembled them, “in all tones and similaritie.” The
Fatherhood sough to pacify and divide the classes and organization was
th easiest way to do so.

The Fatherhood dictated these edicts were to emphasize equality over all
people; all people were treated equally and therefore were to be
segregated equally as well as equally enforced to look alike to all
other persons. The brutal logic found credence in the worrisome lack of
supplies and food; that never reached the war efforts.

Schooling was expected of all persons, every person was schooled to
their assessed limitations based on genetic tests imposed onto little
children who did not know that their birthdays were also their judgment
days for the remainder of their lives. Many saw only a few months of
education, WHile others seemed to disappear into the Fatherhood’s
Schools of Service.

Whole lives were decided by a needle’s prick and a drop of blood.
Careers were applied for; not by a proposal between an employer and a
prospective employee, but rather worker cards were issued with degrees
of skills assigned by the color of the card. Some lives were determined
long before the blood hit the sensor.

Tony’s card was colored blue, which means he was unskilled due to him
not being selected for higher expectations of schooling. Little words
and some counting; enough to listen for a calm and and the ability to
remember how many rounds were in one’s rifle - basic comprehension and
math skills.

Red cards meant you held higher levels of understanding and those were
given work in Fatherhood buildings.

Yellow sent you to the service guilds. Some moved things from place to
place. Others stamped paperwork. Yellows wore badges that informed the
reader of their speciality.

Blue cards meant your muscles were of more value to your fellow man than
your brain could ever be.

A blue, that day Tony was in the northeast quadrant, assigned to service
a Fatherhood building. Every building carried cracking scars along their
facade and flanks. Offices, Service Centers clogged with paperwork
workers sifting through piles while mired in ever more paper, every
day - unending flows of refined papyrus.

Today, Tony was spooling cables out of a wing in the reclamation
process. out of the window of the sixth floor Tony paused for a break as
another crew of workers hollered to reset and take a break.

Out the window the streets were patchy with ancient and newly poured
piecrust asphalt. But the Pedwalls worked and were maintained to the
highest respect. The Fatherhood’s one enduring cultural-directive was
the Pedwalls – the division and separation of the persons in this city
was their only accomplishment.

The Blox were ever present. Outside the Fatherhood’s Building and
inside. Smaller fist sized to ones little bigger than toasters hummed
and darted overhead. Small maintenance Bloxes welding and cutting
material, while workers like Tony positioned themselves to catch parts
and portions of paneling to be reused elsewhere.

Outside the glass affixed to the buildings was filthy from the constant
winds and scratched by the sands blown in from the barren fields
surrounding this broken city.

The steel, wherever exposed, was malformed and too thin to support the
weights, but by some strange happenstance of nature the buildings
remained standing although they groaned in the heavy wind and strained
with the weight of footsteps within the hallways.

Looking out the dust caked window the diagonal line of one numbered
street bisected a named street. What they said or counted was of little
import to Tony, he sipped a cup of water to his lips and enjoyed the
chance to sit and rest.

A scream shot through the worksite. Outside the window Tony faced a rush
of something dropped from above and was gone in a blink.

Silence sucked the air from the scene.

Now shaking, he inched toward the glass to see down to the street.

He recoiled from the crashed broken sight of another worker against the
pavement below. The awfulness drew workers out from windows and perches
to see. Tony heard a similar scream once - as a man fell off the side of
a docked aircraft carrier, from Tony’s first war.

Once is enough when you hear it; and you never forget the sound of man
falling to his death.

As the tram bumped and trundled across Fatherhood Mall, Tony sat quiet.
Watching the monuments pass him by then the Bloxes patrolling and
swooping to and from the Fatherhood headquarters.

In Tony’s throat, he couldn’t swallow it nor spit it out; something was
there. A tinge at first then then the sting of bile, a burp rolled
through his chest, throat, to then press out of his teeth puffing his
lips as it left him.

The whole world seemed alien and just as it could only be. He swallowed
trying to push the burn back down his esophagus, but it didn’t go away
and stayed the whole commute back to his HomeBlock; the uselessness of
it all.

“Order and control is the visage of the past’s painful toil.”

Unknown.

The Great Recycling 

A cultural revolution inclusion from the Fatherhood -like the Pedwalls.
The Blox were black cubes that floated on crude hover-fans and blinks
red, green, and orange lights. They had little real power, save either
staining you with a red spray that would incapacitate you and make you
sleep. Other times they would simply spit at you a tag fired from a
barrel hidden in the center of their steel bodies. They watched
everything, like shiny black bulldogs standing guard; they watched all
the time and never blinked—an old cultural directive from the
Fatherhood.

Blood flooded the streets, the frightful and awful night the Great
Recycling began. Battalions of troops filled and commanded the
intersections and spaces. There was not a place for a citizen to go
without their work papers or passes for any slight discrepancy. Those
days saw a reforging of this world. From one of people to one of lines
and sections, with forbidden zones.

Once left free natural state, the world previous to this one held
strange beliefs and ceremonies. Rooted in that free world, this world
was cut and grafted by the Fatherhood’s Commanding Orders and the
description of long quelled grievance torn open fresh. The Cultural
Evolutions formed a painful decimation of this world. cording off
sections of the city for uses and prohibition of use.

After the troops filled the intersections, Bloxes descended out of the
sky plaguing the people like locusts did the Egyptians.

HomeBlock Orders uprooted whole blocks of families and residences
casting them to far flung corners of the city. Expected to “settle”
their contribution to the Fatherhood’s Great Recycling.

Shambled buildings foisted on new residents as old unfaithful ones were
removed. The apartments still held the remnants of former lives, now
gone and new ones in their place.

Dwellings now declared a paradise; reclaimed from the heathen world. In
their pacification and reclaiming, buildings and whole neighborhoods
were left stripped of all wiring and useful fixtures. Material carted,
carried, and cursed along orders demanding action shifted the face of
the city. Uses and functions followed the people as they forced round
pegs into square holes and cope absurdity.

Much of the absurdity arrived out by men like Tony. Blue Laborers.
Seemingly meaningless laborious tasks were ordered and countermanded
within the whims of cryptic orders from Fatherhood Homeoffice.

In only a short amount of time, the city lay clad in high fencing around
Administration buildings. Sections of the city are no longer passable
without clearances. The efficiency promised and the fraud to be carved
out of their society never materialized. But rarely was this even seen
by the Labor class. Trimmers caught glimpses of these places but even
they were not needed or wanted in the Administration and Fatherhood
Buildings

Soon the Fatherhood Members were barely seen in the homeblocks and
rarely without a grip of Bloxes hovering nearby. The few members that
slummed their time in the homeblocks did so out of addiction, lust,
love, even at times violent reasons.

The dumpster was nearly filled. Tony had shouldered an armful of copper
wiring over the high edge. Momentum helped him thrust it upwards, but
gravity forced the wire coil back on top of him. Bloxes sped to crowd
around the disruption.

Now, laying down with the wiring tugged off him by other laborers.
Bloxes whirled and beeps transmissions while reporting back to their
respective data centers. Alerts noted, recorded, and already logged into
Tony’s “permanent file.” Every resident of the city was tracked,
journaled, archived and chronicled. A medical Bloxe hovered near Tony
casting a focused light into each eye one by one.

CONTUSION. SOFT TISSUE BRUISING. ONE-AND-ONE-HALF DAY REST.

Crackling a string of numbers after its diagnosis and treatment plan
printed to paper expelling itself from a slot at its base - like a
necktie dangling.

The paper trimmed itself and dropped on Tony. Security Bloxes droned
away after the Medical Blox floated off.

Still dazed, Tony felt a sharpness coming into his eyes. The printout
ordered a rest of one and one half work day.

The tram wobbled and rolled down the street as it crossed the main
plaza. Cradling his head and leaning against the window, Tony saw the
massive marching grounds unfold. The last green maintained space in the
whole city. Fatherhood Members crisscrossed out of and into the
buildings skirting the plaza. And just as his eyes focused on the scene
the rush of a grey wall cut his line of sight as the tram fled back into
the concrete and dim glow of the city.

Blocks began to remind him. He started to recognize the homeblocks.
Swinging his hand toward the stop request; chiming it just after the
stop Tony sought.

Six blocks later the tram trundled to a stop. Tony gripped the handrail
as he followed the steps and alighting to the curb. The movement and
rush of his blood pressure sounded and boomed from inside his skull.

Now, off the tram and exposed to the bright afternoon sun. Turning once
and squinting at street numbers he was almost lost right before he
remembered the street numbers leading back to Naim Street, not his
HomeBlock..

 

Not Your Fatherhood’s Peanut Butter 

Like everything that a man can put to his hand and mouth, peanut butter
is subject to abuse.

Two men in thick brown trench coats stood in a dilapidated warehouse on
the city‘s southern side, the taller one - Tony - towered over the
shorter, Willie Menendez. Their coats pulled tightly to cover them from
the whipping cold, looking like thin stage curtains as if the men were
warring playhouses. They were sharing a small jar of a golden-brown
muddy substance - peanut butter - long since outlawed by a cultural
evolution years past. And like any drug the desire inflated the price,
which created a blackmarket, which created an underground economy.
Ending in the transaction that brought us to these two men. Their spoons
clashed like swords at a duel for the remnants of this coveted, illicit,
illegal luxury.

The peanut butter wasn’t relative to the type that children cry for on a
Saturday afternoon on bread with jelly accompanying, it had many
additives along with its mystique - things to burn the brain into
hallucinations and terrible states of sensation. And though it was
labeled for its lovely brownness and creamy texture, it had nothing to
do with the ancient food.

As with any drug - and this was a sinister drug, the price of admission
includes losing something in exchange. When consumed, one can expect
nothing detrimental in the short run, but sneaking terrors come with
extended consumption. Many times news spread via mouth and ear about
desperate souls never awaking after consuming excess amounts in efforts
to sleep.

The warehouse they stood in was demolished on the western side caved by
neglect. Graffiti stained the walls. A rat waddled near a pile of broken
bricks and scrap metal. Many of these debris piles sprinkled the puddled
concrete floor. Not long before the last war, the building was a
bustling warehouse filled with goods and products of a vibrant economy.
Now merely a broken shell of itself.

“Can you believe they made this stuff illegal?” Willie chirped with his
mouth gummed with peanut butter.

“This batch burns a little when you swallow too much,” Tony said.

Willie replied, “Yeah, but it burns so…very…good.”

“We’ll have to burn the jar.”

“Nah, man. You can return the empties back and get a little off the next
jar.”

“Really? How long have you been at this?” Tony aghast at the braggart.

“Oh, I’ve been on this wagon for a while now,” Willie spoke proudly.

“Where do you get it man?” Tony wondered aloud.

The Willie’s eyes narrowed and his weight shifted to one side as he
leaned closer to the other.

“Why you wanna know?” His eyes fixed in a tense stare. “What are you…a
Snipe for the Blox? What’s with all the questions? You wearing tags?”

“Nah, man, I ain’t no damn Blox lovin Snipe! Punk them putas! Take it
easy then - you’re ruining my buzz.” Tony didn’t mean to sound harsh,
but he was tired and his headache had been drilling between his ears.
After some peanut butter, the ache dulled and he was left warm inside,
with his skin slightly buzzing and fuzzy.

The two men stood in an awkward silence until the incident slid off them
like cold butter out of a hot pan when the trill and beeps of a Blox
nearby.From what direction they could not tell.

The moment froze the men solid. The beeps and whirl grew closer and
slifghtly quicker.

Neither man dared breathe until the trills and beeps grew distant.

They sighed in unison.

Another pass of each other spoons in and out of the jar they shared.

A moment later, the jar now empty. Thin streaks and grooves of the brown
goop remained. Neither man’s spoon was able to scrape any more.

“You ever catch a bad batch?” Tony spoke with a hint of nervousness in
his throat.

“No, I don’t worry about that, man. I got me a good supplier. Her stuff
is the best, “ Willie responded.

“Icy. Listen Willie, I gotta get going. Back under the bus, ya know?”
Tony paused to examine the face once more, “See ya around man.”

“Yeah, later, Tony. See ya, man.”

Tony was already a few steps away in the street; ruffled his green-brown
coat against the cold gusts.

Trespasser on HomeBlock 114

Tony’s leg braces creaked as he walked and whined like a fearful dog
with every step taken. The ache within his right knee carried a general
pain, but with the cold the steps could stab all the way up through his
lower back.

The intended to support his implanted knee and femur, while also
supporting his barrel chested frame. The braces in fact did not serve
any real effective purpose while they creaked and squeezed his knees;
Tony mouthed his cursing in their direction as they began to dilute his
buzz.

Tony stopped next to a lamppost. It towered over Tony’s head like some
obtuse rectangle with only three sides at the bottom but following a
slow twist into four sides at the top; as if it was some geometrical
mishap that a god had stabbed into the ground to curse the earth.

The lamppost was covered up to eight feet with scraps of paper and
posters colorful blocks of paper with symbols scrawled on them. One was
slapped and tagged at three of its four corners: The Daily Belle, a
“newspaper” but Tony could never bring himself to say it out loud - the
newspapers where far from the ones he could remember before the wars.

The Belle - intended as a newspaper - was the collection of the random
data and “progess” accomplished by the “Fatherhood’s Husbandry of the
People.” The motto lay above the week number and either a pictogram of a
Sun for the morning edition; or a crescent moon for the evening
editions. This one had a ten followed by a string of numbers some inm
two digits and another four didgit hen the moon symbol.

The rest of the paper was in columns some the same widths and others
thicker and thinner, all of them travelled the length of the grey
broadsheet.

A thin column held a string of shapes and triangles and others with
strange lines over a geometric shape thick across with two dipping lines
along the bottom; one mid way and the other resembled the curve of a
banana. There were triangles and circles all in strange angles. Some
covering each other and others spaced far apart; and thoroughly
confusing Tony staring back at it.

He lifted his hand forward to pin the fourth corner to see the last of
the big column - no pictures saying what to do or what it meant; Tony
ignored the small print - it hurt his eyes; the headaches would make his
sight blur and the text of the Belle induced them very easily. The words
tangled themselves with each other the decrees and statements not of the
writers of the words but rather every column began and ended: “For and
By the Fatherood.”

The Fatherhood wrote everything because only the Fatherhood had
unprinted paper. One or was it two wars after Tony’s last, the
Paper/Pulp Directive rationed to those who needed it. Different persons
read different papers because the words printed on lines of the paper
meant different things to the decoding of the persons minds that read
them.

To Tony, this decree meant little and in his mindseye the words twisted
into a large spiral into the center of it creating a blackened patch of
ink that glistened like an eye staring back at the reader.

The angle of the lamppost bent like a reed in a strong wind with the
light beaming out of the top. Shining vertical to the sky, like a
flashlight attempting to blind celestial eyes in the black inky night
sky.

The blackness crept slowly from the opposite end of the earth as the sun
retreated. To Tony, it always seemed strange how there were always more
things outside when the sun dived below the horizon than while the sun
blazed high overhead.

One such creature, Pamela, crept slowly from across the street as soon
as the sun hit the sand way off in the west. She crossed the street
loping like a cat hunting a mouse. A few steps, a pause then hesitation,
then the soft gallop closer all the time never breaking eye-contact with
Tony.

The air of the night licked at Tony’s face from the creeping darkness
surrounding the world right then.

“Hey! Got the time, Shuga?” Pamela’s words birthed out of her mouth with
nothing less than the way moist heat sweats on the side of a dying
battery.

“No, sorry Little Machine. I ain’t got a Ziploc on me.”

“Ahh, Tony I trust you. You is one o’my best toilers,” Pamela pleaded
and bit her lower lip

“Sorry I just don’t got the fare for the ride right now.”

She clicked with her mouth a sound that meant disappointment with Tony’s
judgment and rubbed him with her side like a cat rubbing its scent on a
piece of furniture. She then grinned and strutted along the side of a
building then slipped into the doorway threshold. Two panythosed legs
streched out long from where she slipped away. Tony saw a run stemming
from halfway up her inner left thigh and extended all the way into the
darkness under a short miniskirt. Pamela leaned forward stretching her
back and her beasts floated above the legs extended from the
building-side. Their roundness bulged from a halter top. A spray of
freckles lined across the apex of her right breast now shining in the
glow of the lamplight.

Mouth agape, Tony stayed himself and watched them extend outward and
light exclamations of Pamela’s gasp. The memory flooded his mind faster
than he could think of it.

His mind’s eye cast Pamela laid out under his naked frame. She wore a
leather bodice half-zipped down with the breasts nearly toppling out.
Her abdomen archeed toward him, his arm slipping under the small of her
back; both shifting and locked pushing and pulling each other to
ecstasy.

Each tmepting the little death and release from the world around them.
As the memory washed over Tony the visions grew more vivid.

In his memory, her legs locked around him. He remembered how she smelt
and the sting of pheromones triggering the warm tangle between the
lovers locked together, arms clutching one and another; heavy breathing
and moans echoed in his mind.

The rumble of the Pedwalls rising began shaking Tony from his daydream.

Panic washed over Tony’s mind as he was too far away from his street. He
had to retreat back to his dwelling before the walls came up. He bolted
toward his block cutting across the intersection .

“Shake that Ass, SUGAH!” Pamela jeered as he hurdled the first pedwall -
one block; three to go.

The tops of walls rose every night to the height of twelve “peds” high;
this being the word for these walls dictated by the Fatherhood. The
Pedwalls were under the street at every intersection and divided the
city equally separating the visible differences of the buildings facades
and the inhabitants that resided there in. The walls also served another
function; they rendered the streets impassable making the cover of
darkness a less likely avenue of escape from this broken city. The
Pedwalls lowered each day for labor. and raised at the end of the labor
day; leaving the evening hours idle, but seperate each street separated
form the next; no way to congregate; no chance to know your neighbor
more than the color of coat or coveralls they wore. The walls sealed the
blocks off from each other rendering moving or mobilizing in darkness an
impossibility.

The streets passed under his feet; the braces whined with the pace of
his stride. Clicking at an ever-increasing pace on his wrist, a duration
counter whirled as Tony walked quickly in the direction of his dwelling.
But Tony stopped mid stride at the sight of her fiery mane bouncing and
dancing as she crossed his path. The curls of her red hair glowed
strangely before his eyes and with some unseen gravity pulled him closer
to her. His motivation could have been the strong aftertaste of the
peanut butter in his mouth, perhaps the effects of the illicit drug were
now pulsing through his brain and in his veins but he could not help the
urge to call out to her.

“Miss! Excuse me, Miss? I feel as though I am a bit lost,” Tony lied a
small and insignificant white lie. He knew exactly where he was and
where he had to go. But for some reason he stopped running.

“How is it?” The woman turned to him and spoke with a smooth supple tone
that carefully slid into Tony’s ears like honey dripping from a comb,
“To feel lost, you are just the opposite of found.”

Tony sapproached her as he contemplated the words given him by her. The
words ached into his ears - her voice caught in a loop repeating in his
head as and a smile crept across his face. He had not heard words spoken
in the manner she had just spoken to him. The smile was a product of two
factors, one from the delightful play of her words and two the obvious
lie that he was currently caught in.

The look reflecting back into his gaze was of a different manner—it
would have made a clicking sound if it made any sound at all—it would
have smelt like a flower in the sun if it produced any smell—it would
have felt like a fine crushed velvet on the nerve endings ones genitalia
if it could initiate any such sensation—it would have tasted like a fine
wine in the mouth of a man who was to never taste its sweetness again.

In her eyes, she saw a tall, broadly built man. Large strong hands swung
with his stride like opposite pendulums of some strange clock. The clock
mechanics composing his muscle covered shoulders and the various tissues
and veins all well oiled by the blood pulsing just below the surface—all
barely visible by a smooth outline pressing through his thin dark brown
coat. Her green eyes seemed to glow in the dark as she marveled at the
owner of those eyes, large hands and powerful shoulders. She marveled at
the beauty she found in this almost alien creature to her as it was not
normal for her to see men of his shade.

The cream colored skin glowed from below the cascading fiery red hair
that still pulsed with some strange gravity pulling Tony closer.

“Miss, is this N..Niam Street?” Tony struggled to pronounce the street
label as he marveled at this strange new creature before him.

“Whatever it is I believe we are trapped here now,” She motioned to the
white marks slowly rising out of the concrete, ped by ped, on both ends
of the street. “Looks like we’re both in this for the long haul,” her
frankness echoed through Tony and she reassured him with a playful grin.

The Pedwall ceased rising and now trapped them for the evening. He had
slept out before; but he was worried he wouldn’t find a place to keep
from being sprayed by a Blox.

“My label is Tony,” he gestured with the tips of his thick fingers
stretching out towards her.

The delicate yet strong looking fingers she produced from the confines
of her coat slowly, after a moment of hesitation, stretched out to the
close proximity of his.

Their fingertips mutually shook at the apprehension and in anticipation
of the touch and finally with only an inch of space between them a blue
arch of light sparked. A shock ran through their nerves and dulled the
uneasy sensation of talking to a complete stranger. The shock ran up
their bodies and ended just behind their eyes as they stared deeply into
the mirror-like glassiness of each other’s eyes.

“The Blox will be flying over our heads soon. We better get off the
street,” her sober clarity of their situation injected reality into the
dream Tony was sure he was having. His head swam as he followed behind
her lovely figure to a stairwell that seemed to lead up infinitely into
the heart of a building.

Tapping lightly on the worn granite steps her feet carried her up—Tony
followed quickly behind her his feet making a loud thumping tone. Her
soft fingers manipulated the handle of the door of her dwelling and
pushed the thin door, swinging it open; she glanced back to see Tony
nervously in tow. She paused there standing in the doorway and touched
her thumb to her forefinger remembering the blue spark of light they had
shared.
