A pen and sheet of paper appeared in the glass box and a lazy-susan spun
clockwise and opened to Reggie.

The message had to be short and quick. A code, but no too obscure.
Simple, but direct. Another nail struck Reggie between the eyes.

He scribbled the message, a time, then his name.

“Here, my friend,” Reggie spun the lazy-susan around. “Please send it to
the number at the top.”

The clerk took the paper and shrugged as he approached the ancient and
yellowed fax machine.

The beeps were rhythmic as the clerk punched the phone number on the
dial-pad. The paper fed into the machine and the machine shuttered into
life. The abrasive sound of dial-tone shifting into warbled frequencies
and finishing with a wash of static somehow reassured Reggie that this
would work. It had to work.

“That’ll be $2.75.” The clerk turned to find a five dollar bill in the
lazy susan. Reggie did not have anything smaller; he hoped the extra tip
would buy silence but remain small enough to be unmemorable if a guy in
a black suit showed up asking questions.

###

Analog transmissions were outside the regular scope of tracked
transmissions. They were more difficult for computers - digital
machines - to intercept, analyze, and render. Also, they were slowly
becoming the dinosaurs of communication systems. Reggie did not know
this, but an old copper wire was his only way of communicating with the
interconnected digital world. Reggie drove his car slowly and obeyed
every traffic law. He needed to buy time before his rendezvous.

###

Target is loose. Outside of sandbox. REPEAT. Target is loose.

###

The blinds of Jessica’s office remained closed all day. House
Representative offices are notoriously small and cramped; almost
inhumane. Inhumane because staffers spend close to 18 hours a day in
less than two thousand square feet. That is not the inhuman part. It’s
the six by eight space that the staffer is usually allowed to have a
desk, computer, and a phone. Lucky staffers got their own work-prison
cell. The unlucky ones had to commandeer the front waiting area coffee
table to make their fundraising calls, do their writing, and conduct
research.

The day was over. Jessica was fuming over the wild traffic of pings,
emails, messages for comment, and pure insanity pouring into her
secure-link. She had a headache. The first one in three years.

Fuck you, Reggie. Fucking AWOL son-of-a… She did not have the energy to
continue hating Reggie right then.

Then Jessica heard something she had not heard in ages. The cacophony of
beeps and washing-electronic sounds was undeniable. She froze at her
desk. She wiped her hand across the air in front of her and away went
all the insanity of her secure-link’s desktop. Her gaze focused on the
one machine rattling and stirring to life just beyond her office door,
the fax machine.

The paper spitting out was one sheet, written by a hand she intimately
knew.

You son-of-a-bitch. She read the instructions, cryptic yet,
understandable. She snatched her purse from a drawer in her desk and
retrieved her coat as she rushed out of her office.

###

It was a short walk from her office. Watch your six. The message was
clear, paranoid, but crystal clear. She stuck to the alleyways but it
was only a few more steps.

“Keep walking,” The voice was familiar. “Stop in three steps and check
the strap on your shoe.”

“Where the hell have you been all fucking day?” Jessica grumbled as she
stretched down, feeling the pull in her hamstrings, and fiddled with the
strap on her $200 dollar Jimmie Choo shoes; these shoes were the polar
opposite of functional, but the Hill does not care about functionality.

Even with one good eye, Reggie noticed Jessica still had it, and in all
the right places. He saw her only yesterday, and yet he felt like it had
been years.

“I’m sorry, but I’ve had a shit-sandwich of day, too.” Reggie answered
in a muffled voice - his hand over his mouth. “It’s no excuse, but shut
off your secure-link before you look up and I think you’ll understand.”

Jessica stood up straight, eyes closed, switched off her secure-link,
and huffed, “How could I possibly…”oh she opened her eyes, “Oh Fucking
Christ!”

“Shh, shh. It’s not as bad as it looks.”

“Looks pretty fucking bad from here,” Jessica was not the most motherly
of individuals which was an asset on the Hill.

“We’ve got to get off the street. Quick.” Reggie could not help how
crazy and paranoid he sounded at that moment. “I’m parked around the
corner. Here’s the keys. Bring the car to me. Please.”

Jessica took the keys. Her mind flooded with questions as she tapped the
unlock button on the remote. She stepped to the driver’s door and the
door unlocked. “You’ve got a shit-ton of explaining to do,” she growled.

But they could not speak at that moment. The car had a microphone linked
to the entertainment and navigation system; an easy hack for any novice
to listen in on the conversation - their mutual paranoia kept their
drive mute.

###

Reggie spun around and locked Jessica’s kitchen door as soon as they
entered.

“Turn everything off. I’ll wait here.” Reggie said in a hushed tone. His
one eye darting around the room - his bandaged eye had grown Pinker from
the blood loss.

Jessica obeyed and walked through her townhouse shutting down everything
that had a wireless transmitter, which was virtually every appliance she
owned. She turned around and said, “What now?”

“Unplug it all,” Reggie’s eye-socket ache. “Even the fridge.”

“Fuck! Seriously?” Jessica said but honestly believed Reggie’s paranoia.
She jerked on the fridge enough to reach her arm behind the refrigerator
to pull the plug.

“Wait, that’s a pre-Internet-of-Things model, right?” Reggie noticed the
lack of a touchscreen on the front panel door.

“Yeah, plugged or pulled.” Jessica was losing patience.

“Leave it.” Reggie sucked his teeth. “The basement. Now.”

As they descended the stairs Jessica jabbed, “Are you going to tell me
what happened to your face?”

“I had to tear it out.”

“You what? We’ve got to get you to hospital! Why the hell did you do
that?” Jessica registered a note of maternal empathy.

“I had to pop it out with a spoon,” Reggie confessed, even now he
realized how insane that sounded. “It wasn’t my first choice. But it was
the only option.”

“Why the hell would you do that?”

“It was counting down and heating up.” Reggie’s hands began to shake. “I
freaked out,” his voice cracked. “It was all the things I remember
reading about before they implanted the device around the ocular nerve.
Remember all those stories we heard about people's heads catching fire?”

“Shh, shh,” Jessica reached for Reggie. He recoiled and drew away, then
relented to the safety of her arms.

“I just don’t know what’s going on.” He began to sob. “Today,
everything…” His voice trailed off as he pressed into her shoulder.

“It’s gonna be okay,” Jessica lied, just as everyone else would have
done in this fragile moment.

They held each other and both began to cry.

Reggie’s empty eye socket stung with the salty tears.

###

The lovemaking was instinctual. A primal reaction to stress and
confusion, but still genuine and heartfelt. It was in the basement of
Jessica’s townhouse that each of them tried to flush away the remnants
of that day with just the right amount of energy, blood, and passion. It
washed over them, like a cleansing hot bath, giving them a moment's
reprieve from the hell that chased after them.

###

“You’ve got one in your head?” Reggie asked.

“I know. But I know more about it than you do.” Jessica lay in Reggie’s
arms. Both of them naked and vulnerable.

“What about it?” Reggie asked.

“The uplink is easily fooled,” Jessica just accessed the bios of her
secure-link, carefully and methodically, she probed the ports and
sub-protocols.

“It’s just a matter of switching off the right switches.” A confirming
beep in her ears reassured her. She looked into Reggie’s eyes and kissed
him.

“It’s gonna be okay.”

“I know when you’re lying,” Reggie smirked.

But Jessica was actually lying. Her vitals were scrolling across a
corner of her heads-up display. Reggie's were scrolling across the other
side of the display. It was helpful a few moments earlier when both
their heart rates and respiration were in-sync and peaking at a very
convenient moment.

They both raised up and sat beside each other, naked, together, but
still alone.

“What’s next?” Jessica dared to break the silence.

“There are files in the secure-cloud that we have to get a hard copy
of,” Reggie sucked in a deep breath and sighed into his hands.

“The ‘papers’?” Jessica probed.

“Yes,” Reggie sucked his teeth. “Those, but also another set. Remember
the debates a few years back?”

“The secure-link?”

“Yes,” His heart was heavy. “There is something in his notes, in the
research, the backgrounds that he was trying to warn us about. Something
that is bigger than the both of us.”

Reggie hated to sound desperate but the day’s events would not allow for
anything else.

“I can get them right now,” Jessica offered.

“No,” Reggie’s eyes flashed with fear. “No! Don’t you fucking dare.”

“Ok, OK!” Jessica had almost called up the link, but her instincts were
right.

“We have to do this the old-fashioned way,” Reggie cautioned. “Quietly.”

“We can get into the office right now. But it’s going to be tricky
getting the documents without a digital trail.”

“What if we, you know,” Reggie began to ask.

“Do you know what you are suggesting?”

“Yes, and no...shit, what the hell is fucking going on?!”

“Shh, shh,” Jessica wrapped her arms around Reggie before he could
recoil. “The right thing. The right thing.”

###

“If what you are saying is true, “ Jessica said. ”Then we have more to
worry about than just what’s stored in the office cloud.”

“Yeah, I know.” Reggie sunk back into the couch. Jessica had found an
old t-shirt of his and pair of cargo shorts he had left over once before
and given them to him. She wore her Air Force tank-top and a pair of
jogging pants.

Through a small window in the basement, light from a street lamp shown.

“The real debate is how and when do we do what we need to do?” Reggie
said what they both were thinking.

“I can get in and pull the backup,” Jessica said after a long pause
between them. “Won’t take me five minutes once I’m inside.”

“They’ll see the data pull. You’ll be tracked and dead before you get
back here.”

“I helped design and test this fucking thing,” There was venom in her
voice. “They take my shit and try to kill you and do in fact kill Baker
with it! Fuck them. I’m not lying down on this one.”

She tightened the laces on her running shoes on the arm of the couch
then she reached behind the couch. Her hand blindly searched for a key
on a nail embedded in the wooden frame of the couch. She pulled it from
behind the couch and it glinted in the light.

“Do you think you have it in you to do something the old-fashioned way?”

“What, with a keyboard? What the hell are you suggesting?” Reggie was
puzzled.

“I’ve got something for you,” Jessica flipped the key in the air, caught
it, and walked across the room to a locked cupboard. “Old hackers die
hard.”

She pulled a padded messenger bag from the cupboard and brought to
Reggie.

“The password is my favorite ice cream with the vowels replaced by
numbers and symbols,” She said as the messenger bag hung between them.
“It’s failsafe-encrypted. If you guess wrong too much, the hard drive
fry's itself like bacon. But it doesn’t smell as good.”

Reggie took the bag. Jessica walked toward the back door exit of her
basement. It would lead her to the alleyway. From there it was a short
mile jog to the office. Nothing out of the ordinary, staffers often
jogged into their offices late at night. This wouldn’t raise suspicion.

Fuck, what’s her favorite ice cream? Reggie was alone with this
conundrum as Jessica stretched her sides, then legs, and began a slow
jog down her alleyway toward the Hill.

###

Vanilla. What? Really? have you ever seen her eat a scoop of vanilla ice
cream? Reggie shook off the idiotic thought.

The computer screen glowed as Reggie sat with it on his lap, on the
couch, in Jessica’s basement.

Butter Pecan. Numbers and symbols replacing the vowels. B. … … Reggie
scanned the keyboard for numbers and alternate symbols looking for
anything that resembled a U. Okay, not butter pecan.

Cookie Dough. That should be simple enough. Zeros for the O’s.
Exclamation point for the I. The number three for the E.

Reggie stopped cold. Three tries or its a baconized hard drive. She’s
never eaten Cookie Dough ice cream in front of me. Reggie stopped and
closed the lid of laptop. You idiot, she’s wanting you to snoop around
her place.

Reggie ascended the basement stairs. The wood creaked under his weight.
He had only ever been in Jessica’s townhouse twice before. Once dropping
her off after a happy hour and the other was for their own happy hour.
The basement door opened to the hallway. Her townhouse was minimalist.
Clean and elegant. She did not have many pictures of family. No pets.
But she had books. Real books. Not a databank. Real dried pulp, glue,
and binding...on shelves. The blinds were drawn, there was not an
electronic appliance on; no green or red lights. Reggie pulled plugs out
of the walls.

What, am I paranoid now? Your eye did explode this afternoon.

Reggie even unplugged the microwave.

The freezer. Ice cream. He swung the door open to find a half a tray of
ice cubes. “Really? Jessica! Come on, woman! Why are you making me work
for it!”

###

Jessica had jogged this route a hundred times before. She knew every
seam in the concrete along the way. Every upward disruption where
underneath the roots of the trees had undermined the pedestrians path.
Even the loose and missing cobblestones and bricks. D.C.’s roads and
sidewalks were a horrible patchwork of materials in various states of
decay and disrepair.

She made it a point to never engage her secure-link when she jogged.
This was the only time she ever obtained a reprieve from the constant
barrage of endless communication from work and the world.

###

Subject one is off-grid. Subject two exhibiting no unusual patterns.
Currently exercising along a predictable pattern; well within Sandbox.

###

Sandbox is a term used to describe the theater of operation where a
target or operation occur. Here, the Sandbox was the Capitol Hill
neighborhood adjacent to the House of Representatives office building.

###

Her brow was sweaty and her breathing was labored. She rather enjoyed
that run. She took a couple of the normal turns, but another couple that
she had not done in sometime.

Jessica began to switch on the functions of her secure-link. She was
hesitant to do so, but had to take the risk, it was the only way to
access the secure-cloud while at the office.

She waved to the Capitol Hill Police Officer as she scanned her wrist’s
biochip. The electronic lock on the staffer entrance buzzed and door
gave way to her push.

Soon she was in her office. And the messages flooded her secure-links
inbox. She had to act quickly, but not too quickly as to raise any
suspicions. She began with answering some press inquiries with a set of
canned responses. The Late Rep. Baker’s staff will remain vigilant in
this time of…

###

Ice cream. Of all the things that she could have asked me to figure out
about her. Reggie stood in Jessica’s spartan kitchen. It was obvious
that Jessica did not cook much. The white stove was spotless. The
counters were retro tile - no granite for her. An island in the center
of the black and white checkerboard tiled floor served as both a
prep-station and a table; two bar stools - by the squeak of the
bearings, one was used more than the other. The cupboards were a light
cream with windows - no need to go through them. What did she eat? There
was hardly any food in the fridge. The trash.

He retrieved the trash can from under the sink and a smile spread over
his face. He winced when his cheek muscles nudged his wounded eye
socket. Chocolate.

Reggie still had the empty half-pint ice cream container in his hand as
he slipped and slid down the stairs. He flipped up the screen and
noticed the glowing backlit keyboard.

C...H...0...C...0...L...@...T...3... He hit the enter key right when he
thought he had not spelled it correctly.

The screen dimmed and brightened with a visually stunning
instrument-like panel filled with calculations and readings,
measurements of bandwidth frequencies and spectrum analysis. His one eye
almost could not comprehend the information pouring into it. The data
was twice as much as the secure-link regularly displayed.

Reggie was puzzled by the functions of this operating system. It was
alien, but familiar still. After studying the layout for a moment, he
gathered enough nerve to begin clicking on icons that felt familiar.

Soon he discovered that this was not just a laptop and Jessica was not
just some “technical liaison.”

###

I hope you’re as smart as I think you are, Reggie. She called up a
protocol from a her deep-file and gave the execute command. Suddenly,
the servers and network equipment in her office buzzed and chirped to
life. Through her secure-link she saw the data flowing from the servers
via the wifi signal wavelengths. The purge of the “secret” files she had
been keeping was underway through a RSA-2048-bit with a
triple-hexadecimal hash-key encrypted virtual private network. As data
was copied and transmitted to the only other computer connected to this
protocol she had executed; the laptop currently sitting in Reggie’s lap
in Jessica’s townhouse.

###

WHOA! What’s going on here! Reggie thought to himself.

A download command had opened up and it read: File Purge from Sandbox.
Accept? [Press Enter].

Reggie crossed index and middle fingers of his left hand and pressed
enter with his right hand. The laptop purred and began to download and
felt warm on his thighs.

###

She would not have much time. The data transmission would take at least
twenty minutes; there was a lot of data. She would have to act quickly.
Her skin was cool to the touch as she shivered slightly. She grabbed a
light running jacket she kept on the back of her door. As she swept it
over her shoulders and pushed her arms through the sleeves, she heard
the front door of the office open. Jessica had expected this, but not
this soon.

She pressed against a false panel in the wood paneling of her office
wall. A vertical drawer slipped opened with a knife ready for Jessica to
grab. A good design, if I have to congratulate myself.

Jessica shortened her breaths and listened for the creaking of the floor
and sounds of movement in the outer offices.

No voices. One set of footsteps. He’s 230 pounds. The Capitol Hill
Police Officer at the entrance. Jessica slipped the knife up her sleeve
and purposefully pushed a stack of papers - yes, real paper - and files
off a waist-high filing cabinet next to her window. He would have called
out if he was just making his rounds, she thought.

Footsteps sounded closer to her office door, but no call out. She
decided to play it cool. She’d act like she was gathering up the papers
and have to crouch in just the right way, just in case.

“Ma’am?” A voice called out.

“Hello?” She answered, “Who’s there?” She let a little fear creep into
her voice to play on a man’s natural weaknesses.

“Ma’am? Identify yourself.”

“Jessica Richland. Chief-Technology Officer for Representative…” She
paused and felt a pang of sadness at the realization of the death of her
former boss. “The late-Representative Baker.”

The Officer pushed open the door. Jessica had faux-gathered the papers
into her hands. She felt the knife against her arm inside her jacket
sleeve. He had his hand on his 9mm pistol and his flashlight gripped in
his other hand.

“Sorry to startle you, Ma’am.” The Police Officer released his grip on
the butt of his 9mm. “I mean, Miss.”

“No worries, Officer.” She answered as she rose and set the papers and
files on the file-cabinet. “You didn’t startle me. I was just trying to
clean up a few things.” She lied as through her secure-link the
visualized data as it swirling around them. It was only forty percent
transmitted according to her heads-up display.

“I know, I’ve heard the news. I saw you check-in on the entrance log and
I know that it has been rather...well. I mean.”

“Yeah, well, he was a good Congressman. Loved working for him.” Jessica
did not know why the Officer was still standing in her office doorway.

There was a brief awkward pause.

Sixty-three percent uploaded.

“Well, can I help you with anything, Officer?”

“Davis. Ed....Davis. And no, I’m sorry, I didn’t mean to bother you.”

The data uploaded sputtered and sputtered with numbers jumping by two’s,
five’s, and six’s.

“Well, thank you officer for checking on me. I am fine. Just picking up
a few things.”

Seventy-five percent uploaded. Go along your way, Cop.

“Oh, ok. Well, if you’re on your way out? I could escort you if you
wanted. My parents are from Rep.,” He paused. “I mean, the late-Rep.
Baker’s district. They moved there from Queens. That’s where I’m from.
Then the Marines.”

“Oh,” Jessica sighed. “A constituent. Well, I am almost finished up
here, I guess.”

Eighty-three percent.

“I just have to get one more thing in order. I have to...to see if I had
that one file...yes, Here it is.” Jessica feigned a grin. “I just have
to make sure this is on one of the staffers desks for Monday.”

She took a file and waved it in the air as she walked past the Officer
and out of her office toward a random desk next to one of the printers.

Eighty-nine percent.

“Wow, your office does have a lot of paper,” the Officer commented.

“Well, Rep. Baker liked paper. He was a lawyer for twenty years before
he was a congressman.”

Ninety-six percent.

“Oh, I didn’t know that. That’s cool. He must have been a good one.
Shame he died.”

“Yep,” Jessica felt the knife shift in her sleeve. “Shall we?”

“Oh sure, ladies first.”

“I’ve got to lockup,” Jessica grinned.

The Officer sheepishly grinned and walked toward the front area of the
office.

Jessica pulled her office door shut. Ninety-nine percent.

The Officer was standing at ease in the hallway. One-hundred percent.
File Purge Complete. Checking File Integrity. She could walk away from
it now. The rest of protocol would reformat all drives and install
dummy-backups of the regular work files. None of the staff would notice
any difference on Monday.

“Ok,” Jessica chuckled. “You know, you really don’t have to walk me out.
I know my way around.”

“Not a problem at all, Miss.”

His chivalry was nauseating.

They walked in silence until they got to the stairwell that lead down
and to the back staff exit.

“Oh, you know. I think I met Rep. Baker once,” the Officer suddenly
confessed.

“Yeah,” Jessica half-acknowledged the Officer as he began to ramble and
then descended the stairs. “When?”

“I think it was when I first came back from Afghanistan.” The Officer
was young. But the last mission in Afghanistan had been completed for at
least three or four years.

“You don’t seem that old. Afghanistan has been said and done for a
number of years,” Jessica was suddenly suspicious.

“Well, a lot of things happen to a guy over there.” The Officer sounded
like he was confessing. “A lot of painful things. Takes a while to
rebuild.”

“You mean get over?” Jessica prompted.

“No, ‘rebuild’ is the right word.”

They reached the exit door and stopped and look at each other. Jessica
just had to swipe her wrist across the grey sensor box and the door
would open. But her curiosity was too great.

“What do you mean ‘rebuild’?” She instantly regretted asking this
question and not just swiping her wrist across the sensor.

“I need you to swipe out, Ms. Richland.”

“Huh?”

“Your sensor, swipe out.”

She raised her arm and heard the electronic lock buzz; the door popped
open.

“Oh, one thing,” The Officer interrupted her as she stepped into the
doorway. “Didn’t you think anybody else could see the data transfer?”

A black sack slipped over her head. A hand, outside the sack, over her
mouth. Multiple sets of hands and arms wrapped around her ankles and
wrists before she could kick or swing. Her knife clattered on the
cement. She felt the puncture of a needle into her outer thigh and
zip-ties looped over her wrists and ankles. The last thing she heard
before blacking out: “Target in pocket!”

###

The data-checking protocol completed. The desktop blinked and changed to
a window with thousands upon thousands of files.

Files with strange names:

YELLOWBOUNCE.

CHILLYWATER

ECHELON

GILGAMESH

TURBINE

VICTORYDANCE

XKEYSTROKE

PRISIM

CARNIVORE

OMNIVORE

Reggie clicked on one at random, labeled: “PROJECT FILTER.” The screen
flooded with data and information. He began reading about an implant and
the application of nanotechnology to enhance humans for “more effective
espionage and counter-terrorism force projection.” The mission files
read like the liner notes of old vinyl progressive rock albums and
described a story behind the headlines of major assassinations,
political events, and even “natural” disasters of recent memory.

The death of a middle-eastern despot was originally depicted in the
newspapers as a moment when his subject rose against him and publicly
executed him while streaming it live on the internet. The FILTER reports
described operatives hunting him via his physical and digital data.
Metadate form a series of seemingly random phone numbers that contacted
the same numbers are seemingly random intervals computed a eighty-five
percent probability that a particular head of a newly formed
intelligence agency with a vague connection to the former despot was
feeding information about the manhunt for the despot. The phones were
turned on at certain times and via cell-tower triangulation centered in
a sparsely populated area just outside of town thought to be well within
the command and control of the local forces. The FILTER project
determined these signals also coincided with the signal of a satellite
phone that was used once every three weeks. The number the satellite
phone called was to a house in a suburb of London. That house just so
happened to be the daughter of the despot who had publicly denounced him
years before and pledged a life of public service to all the refugees of
her beloved homeland.

The signals intelligence proved a pattern so convincing that OpOne set a
timer on his desktop to alert him when the next call would take place.
He then set the systems to record the audio of the encrypted phone
conversations. After the public key of the satellite phone was cracked
the voice transmissions were crystal clear and the coarse baritone of
the despot was loud and clear. OpOne put it on speaker during a briefing
he personally gave to the Defense Secretary at that time. The Secretary
recognized the despot’s voice as they had met decades earlier and a now
infamous photo was taken of the two shaking hands.

The cellphone triangulation data was so precise they did not need to
look up the address of the safehouse. They had the exact room location
from which the satellite calls took place and the even the safe room
buried in the backyard from which the burner-phone calls were made and
received.

Out of deference to the useful connection with the despots daughter in
London, they chose to raid the safehouse when the despot was in his
“spiderhole” and engaging in a text message discussion with his source.

The despot was dragged from hiding and presented to a mob forming in the
city center. There is where the camera phones bore witness to his brutal
and violent demise.

Reggie’s blood ran cold in his veins as his mind raced back to the
briefing Rep. Baker and he received on this exact event. Every fact and
circumstance presented was purposefully shaded to hide this very
specific data.

 

5 - The User

Adrenaline shot through his veins and he loved the rush. The dopamine
and other pleasure inducing hormones fired across his synapses. More
than a normal energetic high; this was part of the design. The FILTER
supplements and ultimately changes the brain chemistry via the use of
nanotechnology to encourage the production of dopamine and other
hormones when mission objectives are met and exceeded. In turn, the User
learns to and ultimately wants to complete the mission in order to
receive the reward, like a junkie seeking a fix and given the means...at
all costs.

Josh Hendrickson was a normal guy. He had a job that he did not truly
love. A wife that he once loved, but married life and their careers
faded thier mutual bliss. A house in the suburbs. Two cars, a barbecue,
savings accounts with five digits, a retirement plan. Not having
children and high salaries allowed for this last luxury.

They had carved out a nice niche for themselves. Jennifer, his wife, had
a good public relations position with a mid-sized firm with lots of
potential. She studied hard in school and her professional skills were
sharp. It also helped that she was a workaholic. Like many Professionals
in Washington, DC they focused solely on their work and personal lives
were a distant second.

Josh was a “digital” man. He loved technology. His time in the Navy gave
him plenty of exposure to high-level cutting-edge networks and
components and he was trained extensively in all of them that his short
career allowed. He tried out for the Navy SEALS and incurred a medical
discharge when ligaments in his right knee could not keep up. Honorable
discharge and because his commanding officer made a phone call, a cozy
job with a technology contractor for the Navy waited for him. He
flourished at first, but he missed the smells of the ships and
excitement of live fire drills - even the simulated ones. A year out of
the Navy, he found the monotony burdening him every day he swiped his
ID-chip at the front door of his office building. Then he was picked for
a special program - the FILTER Project.

It was high-level research and development. It excited Josh in ways that
felt unnatural to him, but he welcomed the change. In many ways it was.
He excelled in the aptitude tests and after implantation he sparkled.
His performance biometrics reflected this.

Two years ago the project was running full-force. Every expectation and
objective was being fully accomplished and exceeded. The participants
never met each other, but they competed against the other subjects via
anonymous briefing-boards. Josh’s employer encouraged his total focus on
Project FILTER. They gave him bonuses, stock options, and he wanted for
nothing. Every training mission he was assigned came with first-class
airline tickets, car service, and high-end hotels. His employer, Vision
Systems, could be very generous when profitability was increased and
project goals were exceeded by its best operatives.

Josh loved watching his old assignment’s video logs. This most recent
one almost gave him an erection. It was the stealth and undercover of
this one. Also, the location was truly exciting and torn from the
headlines of the day. He remembered reading about the representatives
heart-attack earlier in the day, but he never thought one of his aides
was a spy for the Chinese. He stopped her. He stopped the secrets in his
files from getting in the enemy's hands. Or so his debriefing session
informed him. The tires of the black van with government plates thumped
on the seams of the concrete on the Baltimore-Washington Parkway. They
were heading north, toward Fort Meade, Maryland.

###

There were other Users in Project FILTER who understood the veil between
private and public worlds was thin, and growing more translucent by the
day.

Jax424 was one of these Users. He was a video game fanatic. Played and
beat - within a few hours or days - every game that his parents bought
him for birthdays and Christmas.

His dad once said, “You’re not going to be some couch potato. Every game
you play and beat means time at practice.”

Jax424 was not a “couch potato.” He played lacrosse - all-state and full
athletic and academic scholarship to a small private college in Northern
Maryland. Studied information technology. Graduated and then found work
for one of the numerous technology contractors the United States
Government had in its stable.

Technology was everything in the modern world. It brought instantaneous
communication around the worlds and streams of data flowed from every
living creature. Simply put,Data was everything. Jax424 played with data
at his job; at least that is what his online profiles said his
profession was. He was a network engineer/administrator. He is the man
that is on the other end of the phone when a hyperactive office manager
calls to complain that his email is not syncing or, “the internet is
running slooooow.”

Jax424 also had access to all the dirty secrets of the network and its
users. He knew that Charlie Johnson, an accountant on the fifth floor
liked to take a break around 3:30 every afternoon. Charlie’s break
involved looking up porn on his desktop computer via an incognito
function of his browser that Charlie thought was not going to register
in browser history.

Jax424 knew this vulnerability and would often mirror Charlie’s screen
around 3:33 pm. After seeing the images, Jax424 did not dare switch on
Charlie’s webcam, he did not want to see the expression on Charlie’s
face. Jax424 did not need to take screenshots, he had the data, along
with the tracing information left behind on every machine that a human
interacts with. The time, the duration, the internet protocol addresses
visited, the messages sent and to whom, then what that person did and so
on. The data never lied and told everything.

Charlie’s biggest vice was naked women on his computer screen in the
afternoon. But Jax424 also knew that the company had a very large “slush
fund” that the Chief Executive Officer had not informed the board
about - which he should have according to the by-laws. The CEO had many
secrets and liked to treat his mistress to stays in the company suite at
the W Hotel in downtown Washington, D.C. He knew this because CEO’s
phone calls to his wife’s cellphone preluded a quick call to a cellphone
number in Columbia Heights, D.C.

That cellphone would then travel via the Metro system - Jax424 watched
as the distinct IP address of the cellphone moved and triangulated
through the underground tunnels of the city on the celluar phone
networks and pinging off the open public wifi networks along the way.

It surfaced in Chinatown and according to the speed was traveling in a
car down H street. It would turn after about ten blocks and follow 15th
street south toward the W Hotel.

Once, Jax424 switched on audio microphone to the company cellphone
assigned to the CEO after seeing this pattern and the CEO’s cellphone
followed a similar pattern from his office to the W Hotel.

He heard soft pillow talk.

A male voice and a female voice were discussing the latest episode of a
show about a singing competition for regular people.

Jax424 recognized the show and really liked the single mother waitress
from Arkansas, but it appeared that these two adulterers were found of a
scrappy musician from Seattle. Grunting and sounds of passion soon
followed. Jax424 triggered the camera on the company issued iPhone but
all he saw was the blank ceiling of a hotel room at first.

A female voice from off camera inrupted the grunting noises, Is that the
new phone one with the cool camera features that can switch peoples
faces?

Charlie’s voice answered, Huh? The fucking camera? Really? Right now?

A female voice responded, Yeah! You wanna picture to think of me in your
office later, right?

Laughter, the camera moved revealing a Charlie’s face, then the view
changed to a naked woman on a hotel bed.

Say cheese! Charlie commanded.

Fuck me! The woman responded and juggled her round breasts in her hands.
A photo snapped and saved to the memory card of the phone and also to
the Jax424’s local hard drive.

Laughter erupted between them.

Let me try something! The woman said and snatched the phone from the
man. Okay, smile!

The camera switched to the man’s face and snapped a photo. The image
saved to the internal memory card of the phone and also a copy to
Jax424’s local hard drive. After few moments of staring at the woman's
concentrating face she grinned and flipped the camera back to Charlie’s
face. An image dropped onto Jax424’s local hard drive.

There! You’ve got my tits!

The image was that of the woman’s body with Charlie’s face imposed over
her’s. IT was awkward to say the least.

Oh hell, no! Charlie shouted and deleted the photo. The copy of Jax424’s
hard drive remained. I need something a little more than that!

The camera switched around to show Charlie’s erect penis about to enter
the woman’s agape mouth. Sucking sounds and moans filled the audio file
being recorded as the shine of the computer monitor glared across
Jax424’s green eyes.

After a few minutes, the camera returned to the ceiling view it began
with as the couple watched the afternoon news on the television. The
video rendered on Jax424’s hard drive and the data began uploading to
Vision Systems private cloud.

Jax424 used his powers benignly. He looked away when their faces entered
the screen, at first. He only really needed to know their patterns - and
by a simple act of deduction their strengths and weaknesses, too.

But Jax424 was eager to move into more advanced projects within the
company and eager to learn more about other branches of the company and
applied research and development of wearable and implanted computer
systems.

Jax424 was nervous he would not get accepted into these programs because
he forgot to spell-check his application before sending it. He remedied
this concern with a quick hack into the Human Resources application
server and fixed a couple of misspellings. He received his promotion and
acceptance papers two weeks later - which he also saw approved a day
before he got notice.

Jax424 was one of the first civilians to receive Project FILTER. Two
days after the bonding process, Jax424 never had to step into the server
room for his job duties. With a swipe of his hands Jax424 had every feed
and connection he needed to the entire information network of his
employer and every target he desired.

Vision Systems immediately saw a three-fold bump in productivity once
Project FILTER was implemented into its Information Technology
workforce.

6 - Paranoia

Time was meaningless when Reggie concentrated on something. His eye
scanned and absorbed the information pouring out of the laptop in front
of him. He did not realize that hours had passed since he last saw
Jessica.

Hunger growled in his belly and shook his attention away from the
glowing screen.

Damn, what time is it? The streetlamp shone through the grimy basement
window giving a soft glow to the room. Where was Jessica? I’m hungry.

Reggie slid the computer off his lap and stood. Stretching and popping
his back and shoulders. He stretched his legs and shook out the achiness
of hours of inactivity.

He ventured upstairs to the kitchen to see if there was anything to eat.

Does she even live here? Reggie wondered as he stared into an empty
fridge. The only items were three kinds of chinese mustard and two
containers of yogurt. The kind with probiotics.

He searched the cupboards, dishes, glasses, cold cereal - but no milk in
the fridge. To the kitchen drawers, nothing but chinese takeout menus
and fortune cookies. Reggie snatched a couple of fortune cookies from
the drawer and unwrapped one as he leaned against the counter.

Do I risk a phone call for a pizza? A brother’s gotta eat! Would they be
listening for my voice?

Reggie’s mind swirled with the day’s events and information he was just
reading. He split the cookie in two and tossed half of it into his
mouth. The fortune strip inside was half-exposed. He pulled it from the
cookie as he crunched the other half of the cookie in his mouth.

“Getting the right answers is only possible when you have asked the
right questions,” the fortune strip read.

Reggie always chuckled at how purposefully vague these “fortunes” always
were.

I can’t risk any communications. I’ll have to find something quick and
pay cash. Reggie remembered there was a carry-out place around the
corner, a memory from a previous visit to Jessica’s townhouse. They had
good noodles.

He would have to make it quick, he did not know where Jessica was or why
she was not back yet.

He threw on his coat and hat and started for the back door. Then he
heard a beeping and his blood ran cold.

Was there something left on? Where they tracking him? Was it an alarm?
Paranoia flushed his veins with fresh adrenaline. Reggie no longer felt
the hunger in his stomach.

He almost stumbled down the stairs to the basement. The laptop chirped
from the couch. He swept it into his hands. A message notification
wiggled on the screen. He clicked it instinctively, but then immediately
thought he should not have.

“They are watching. You are being tracked.”

Reggie’s guts went cold.

The message continued with another entry. “You must move, now. North.
Get to College Park. University of Maryland Campus. Watch for the sign.”

Reggie slapped the laptop shut. For a moment, he paused. Move, you
idiot. Even if this is a trap, you’ll get caught here for sure. It might
be Jessica.

He snatched the black bag Jessica kept this laptop in and slung it over
his shoulder. He grabbed his jacket, his car keys jingled in the pocket.
He could not waste a minute, but he could not move too fast - no
suspicious activity.

The streetlamps glowed in the dark of night as Reggie criss-crossed
through the streets toward the Baltimore-Washington Parkway.

 

7 - The Operators

It was a small room with a design was specially implemented just for
these types of secure hearings. No press ever saw the interior of this
room; no room in front of the committee bench for the photographers to
crawl around. The witness bench was a single row along the back of the
room immeadiately behind the table where Robinson perched himself.

Cell phones were checked with a Marine guard at the outside door. Then
the vistors - only if they held the right color badge and their name was
on the list in the Marine’s heads-up-display were admitted to a small
brightly lit entry room. This room shot a refreshing burst of air on the
entrant. It wasn’t to clear the glisten of humid DC sweat from their
brows, it was a quick burst of air to test for bombmaking materials. The
brightly colored lights were hidding the wide spectrum array of lights
that scanned the entrant for every possible material that wcould either
be a weapon or communications device - transmitting or recording. THis
was the last possible moment that any electronic transmission on any
radio frequency could possibly go out. Whether it was a text message to
a loved one saying, “wish me luck,” or it was a message to a handler on
the outside. Nothing further penetrated the walls after the door’s
electromagnetic seal disengaged and the foot thick concrete door slid to
one side.

This room within a room was a sarcophagus of concrete woven with special
shielding and copper mesh embedded into the walls to prevent
transmissions or signals from entering or leaving. As a by-product of
the sheilding and the concrete, it was also one of the safetest places
to be in the event of a nuclear attack; even a meteor hit was unlikely
to crack its shell.

Inside was where every interior designers dream was murdered in favor of
the brutally boring, plain, and stereotypical late 1970s government
hearing room. The brown and yellow curtains covered faux-windows frosted
and backlit to give the occupants the illusion of daytime and normallcy.
A few high back leather chairs with name plates of Senators infront of
them.

Lieutenant General Jack Robinson sat alone at table, a small microphone
at the center of the table in the hearing room of the Senate Committee
on Special Operations and Projects. SCOM-SOP. Robinson gave the
committee its own command code ward because it was easier to approach it
as something he controlled rather than an oversight committee compelling
him to stand and deliver.

At Robinson’s feet a briefcase; a prop for effect - visually inspected
by the Marine guard outside and electronically scanned by the sensors in
the entrance room. Both instances only a few papers detected. His
portfolio lay open before him, papers marked Top Secret and Classified
in red ink. But useless in reality, he carried all his notes and
briefing papers in his heads-up-display.

Robinson grinned to himself as he remembered getting his first
classified document earlier in his intelligence career. That was a long
time ago, long before the four stars crawled upon his shoulders. His
first classified document was a national security intercept from a
wiretap on a diplomatic cable sent from the Iranian Ministry of Foreign
Intelligence to a small consulate in Lebanon in late-October 1983.

He remembered the words in farsi with the onion paper overlay with an
english translation. The message was short and terse: Send the driver
with the package, praise be to Allah. The driver was a suicide terrorist
meant to drive a large truck laden with plastic explosives and oxygen
tanks. The target was the United States Marines Barracks in Beirut
Lebanon. The intercept itself bore a recieved date of 18-10-83; days
before the bombing occured. On its face the commands for the bombers on
the ground to ready themselves for paradise. The intercept appeared on
Robinson’s desk on Tuesday, October 25, 1983. The bombing occurred in
the early-morning the Sunday before.

Robinson took no pause but for a short gasp to acknowledge the 241
Marines and Servicemen killed in the blast. Bodies still being pulled
from the rubble as Robinson tucked the intercept into a report on the
event that found little relevance until an investigation found faults in
the military chain of command, but levied no accountability. No
accoutability for the myriad of reasons the intercept failed to hit the
desk that could have done anything to prevent it until 48 hours after
the bombing occured.

Back to the present with Robinson and his four-stars standing to
attention as Senators entered from the same entrance via the same Marine
guard. Smiling and laughing about a joke spoken on the other side of the
Marine Guard.

“Jack! Good to see you again,” Senator Richard Burchart was a two-time
incumbent from Missouri. He served in the Army and went to Harvard Law
School. Never set foot in a courtroom during his career as a lawyer.
Then he was tapped by a managing partner at a large firm in Missouri to
run for State Senate. He lost but then the U.S. Senator died in office
and the Governor - a former partner at Burchart’s firm - Burchart had
four years to prove that he could get elected. He did and the money that
went to the now late-senator went to Candidate Burchart. He has never
had a tightly contested election since.

“Good Morning, Senator,” Robinson answered. “How is Nancy?”

“She is well,” Burchart answered. “She’s heading up a
food-nutrition-access thing in Mizzou. Good for the local farmers and
schools from I’ve gathered.”

The other two Senators placed their portfolios next to their red-strap
sealed briefing packets at their positions and offered their hellos.
Robinson returned them.

“Okay, let’s get going,” Burchart said. “Where do we sit on our
projects?”

Robinson began to explain the status of various projects and referenced
handouts and multiple one-sheets the Senators torn open their packets
and began to scan the pages as Robinson spoke.

Robinson spoke in plain and curt statements with short descriptions and
only the high probilities outlined. This was his nature he had honed to
sharp razor like blade he unshealted for these pro-forma “hearings.” The
hearings dictated by the laws that originated the matrix of programs and
commands Robinson oversaw were demanded by the laws enacted by Congress
as defacto oversight and engraved in the boilerplate of the complicated
words on the paper blessed by Congress and given life with signature of
the President of the United States. POssibly the only act that Robinson
was obligated to actually perform - these hearings were just the kabuki
dance required for the ticket to ride and Robinson - and his four-star
shoulders - delivered every one in line, on time, and in perfect
cadence.

“So these projects are going smoothly?” Burchart interrupted Robinson.
An interruption that Robinson expected.

“Yes, the warrants have been provided by the Foreign Intelligence and
Surveillance Court in accordance with the provisions of the Act. The
Department of Justice as well as the attorneys representing the various
agencies have been providing the legally satisfactory and necessary
probable cause to the judges in the FISA court and the warrants and
orders have been signed accordingly by the Judges. We have had no
rejections...”

“General Robinson,” Senator Chuck Ammerstand interrupted the General.
“The probable cause evidence proffered, has it been vetted and examined
independently?”

Senator Ammerstand was a first-term Senator from New Hampshire. He was
elected on a platform of government oversight and reform. “Stand with
Ammerstand” his campaign slogan; and likely going to be the one he used
in his relection campaign. His electorate did in fact, stand with him,
and punched his ticket to Washington with sixty-percent of the vote in
the general election. In the elections these days three points was
considered a commanding victory. Six points is a mandate and ten gives
you room to stomp on some toes. And stomp Senator Ammerstand has in his
short time in Washington.

“Yes, Senator Ammerstand,” Robinson was used to this particular vein of
questions from Ammerstand. “Each aspect of these warrants has been
vetted and examined by numerous levels of United States Assistant
Attorneys as well as heads of departments and cross-oversight by outside
attorneys from other affected departments.”

“Yes, yes,” Ammerstand responded. “But have these requests been reviewed
by a civilian office? Outside of your enforcement and intelligence
structure?”

“We have held true to every requirement within the letter of the
enacting legislation.” Robinson had given this answer to Ammerstand a
number of times before, in previous closed door briefings and hearings.

“I understand that,” Ammerstand shifted in his leather chair and leaned
forward. “But that doesn’t…”

“Chuck,” Burchart interrupted. “We’ve read the reports. You have them
right there. Do we need to explore this?”

“Dick,” Ammerstand raised his hand with the palm toward Burchart. “We
have a responsibility to our constituents to diligently…”

“Yes, yes, I know. I’ve heard your speech about this before. I’m asking
if you have reason to get into the munitae. Right now. I’ve got a
luncheon to get to shortly and I’m sure the General has a busy schedule
to get to.”

Ammerstand sighed. “Ted? Ted!”

Theodore Chaulk was the third Senator in the room. His briefing papers
were left with the red strap still across the pcket; untouched and
pushed to the side of another set of papers he studied more earnestly -
polling information from Georgia. He was behind in very tight primary
race that would be coming to a head in two months. He was being outspent
by radicals in his own party six dollars to every one of his.

“Huh? Yes, I seen nothing troubling here,” Senator Chaulk answered.

Ammerstand sighed and slumped into the back of the leather chair. He
slipped off his glasses and rubbed bridge of his nose.

“Senator Ammerstand?” Burchart probed.

“I’ve got nothing further. I’m not going to get any answers today
anyway. Not here.”

“General? Nothing further to report, if not, I believe that is all we
require of you today. This hearing is adjourned. Thank you, Gentlemen.”

Robinson gathered his papers. He had not even scratched the surface of
the information he brought with him today. He never did. He slipped his
portfolio into his briefcase and started toward the door.

Senator Ammerstand called out to him, “General Robinson? A moment? Off
the record?”

Burchart paused at exit door and huffed. Senator Chaulk had already
barreled out of the room, his briefing papers remained - untouched and
bheind his nameplate in front of his chair. It was normal custom fo the
Senator’s to deposit their briefing papers into the shredder standing
sentry at the exit.

“Yes, Senator?” Robinson paused mid-way to the door.

“I thank you for the time you take out of your schedule to come here and
speak with us on these subjects,” Ammerstand tried to smooth any ruffled
feathers left in the room.

“I would not miss it. It is my duty, Senator,” Robinson answered.
“Anything further?”

Ammerstand paused and contemplated the fight he wanted to pick.

“Chuck? The General has a busy schedule and I’m sure you do too,”
Burchart’s voice was stern, as if he was chiding a young boy.

“Thank you, General. Nothing further.” Ammerstand felt his stomach drop
and an audible sigh escape his lips.

Robinson nodded to Burchart as the exit door slid open; Robinson passed
by with a quick slaute to Burchart.

Ammerstand whipped his overstuffed portfolio under his arm and turned
toward the exit door sweeping up Senator Chaulk’s packet to deposit in
the shredder. Burchart stood at the exit door waiting for him.

“Listen, Chuck,” Burchart said. “We have done our part. Our end of the
process.”

“Dick, I don’t want to hear this speech again,” Ammerstand curtly
responded as he jammed Senator Chaulk’s packet into the shredder.

“Well, you’re going to hear it again. And again. And twice tomorrow!”
Burchart thundered. “I’m tired of you trying to do whatever it is you
are doing or trying to do. I’m the chairman of this committee. I’m the
guarddog. It’s my signature that matters for anything that you think you
will try to accomplish with this kind of posturing. This is your first
time in the dance. I’ll give you that. But I’m not going to accept this
from one of MY committee members.”

The emphasis was on the word “my.” An emphasis that was not lost on
Ammerstand.

“Yes, Senator,” Ammerstand backed down. “Not trying to do anything, just
asking questions where I thought I should.”

“Son,” Burchart said smugly. “Get hot when there cameras on. When it
counts for your friends back home. No cameras here. And the air in here
is the best air-conditioned air you can get in DC.”

Ammerstand’s blood curdled in his veins. He hated this side of being a
Senator - he was never a good actor.

“Besides,” Burchart slid his arm around the shoulder of Ammerstand. “We
have an appropriations hearing next week and there are some bases and
projects on the list that are of interest to your friends back home.”

Burchart was right. There were no cameras to capture the rage. Nothing
to circulate to the voters back home and, more importantly, the donors
to Ammerstand’s next election campaign. Ammerstand came from a
predominately purple state and he would have to posture and dance the
dance for the money to fund his next election. Ammerstand knew he had
win another election if he were to gain any gravitas at these hearings.

###

The laptop chirped from inside the case on the passenger seat next to
Reggie as he drove north on the Baltimore-Washington Parkway. With only
one eye he did not dare try and pull it from the case with one hand. He
pulled off, and once stopped, pulled the laptop and opened it.

“Go to Greenbelt Metro. Park. Take train into DC.”

Reggie shoved the gearshift into drive and headed as directed. He pulled
off the parkway and found his way to the metro station. Once he parked
he walked to the station and stepped onto a train. The last one of the
evening. It was 2:30 in the morning. This was one of the last trains.
Wherever Reggie was going, he was not coming back to this station.

The door chimes sounded and Reggie entered a train car. The laptop
chirped.

“Chinatown. Exit to H Street. Wear the bag on your left shoulder.”

Reggie sat with his wounded eye to the glass. He drummed his fingers on
the laptop and slipped it back into the case. No need to look like and
easy mugging target this late in the evening, he thought.

It’s all uphill from here. He was too nervous to zone out like he and
every other local did while riding the Metro train; it was official,
Reggie was the “crazy-looking-dude” on the train.

###

Reggie stood in the glowing light of D.C.’s sad version of Times Square.
The bag on his left shoulder. He took a deep breath and waited. The
message did not say how long to wait and he did not dare open the laptop
on the street.

A car pulled up next to Reggie. A window rolled down. Reggie expected a
gun to emerge and his part in the play to come to an end.

“Get in.” A voice from the car said.

“What no codeword?” Reggie chuckled nervously.

“Fuck you, that’s my codeword. You want help or not?”

Reggie pulled the handle on the door and entered the vehicle.

The driver shifted the car into gear and drove. The direction did not
matter. The car drove. This was not a commute , it was an interview.

“How did you get that computer?” It was almost not a question - more
like an accusation.

“Jessica,” Reggie said. “She gave it to me then she sent something to
it. I haven’t seen or heard from her since. I’m worried about her.”

“Yeah, me too. She doesn’t drop off.”

The car continued northbound on 7th street. After 7th street turned into
Georgia Avenue, there were fewer streetlamps and more graffiti.

The silence was too much for Reggie, he had to speak.

“Where are we going?”

“To fix this.” The driver said. “She sent us something. We have to look
at it. Also, your bad eye is going to be seen. That’s gotta be fixed.”

“How?” Reggie looked at the driver. “I dug it out of my skull! You got a
magic eyeball machine somewhere? I’ve a quarter for the claw machine, if
you do.”

“You’d be surprised what I got up my sleeve. And I have the best arcade
machine collection. Besides, I’ve seen wounds like that one before,” the
driver said.

“Seen it before?” Reggie was astounded. “How? When?”

“Let’s just say, this ain’t no Uber ride and you’re not the first,” the
driver said smugly. “I’m Trevor. No need for last names.”

“Not the first?” Reggie questioned.

“Been there done that,” Trevor glanced at Reggie. His right eye gleamed
a metallic shine, glowing from the reflections of the night.

Reggie dared not press for details. He presumed Trevor’s story was
similar to his.

Trevor drove the car onto New Hampshire Avenue and into an alleyway
behind a series of rowhouses and pulled into a small carport.

“Let’s go. We don’t have much time.” Trevor yanked the keys from the
ignition and exited the vehicle.

Reggie followed clutching the bag in his arms. It was his meal-ticket.
Without it, he’s a crazy man left on the streets of D.C. to live a
miserable life or die.

Trevor entered through the basement doorway. A scanner clicked and the
door popped open. He disappeared into the blackness inside.

Reggie paused and thought, this could be it. The last doorway I walk
into. A dark room. Will it be a needle to his neck or knife through his
ribcage to puncture his lung?

“You coming?” Trevor called from the darkness.

“Yeah,” Reggie swallowed hard and stepped into the darkness ahead of
him. Curtains brushed against his face, he gasped then saw light split
the darkness. As he pressed forward, the curtains split and he was in a
room of computers and neon light. A surplus army cot sat along a bare
brick wall. Along the remaining walls was a series of large monitors and
Reggie heard the hum of computers.

“Shit!” Reggie exclaimed. “They’ll see us! They track everything!”

“Calm down,” Trevor said firmly. “This is off-grid, it’s a SCIF. Shut up
and take a seat. We’re in a Sensitive Compartmented Information
Facility. The CIA uses rooms like these to keep signals from getting in
or out. YOu’ve been in one before, right?” Trevor asked.

“Yeah, secure briefings,” Reggie confessed. “They always make us check
our cell phones at the door, usually with a Marine sitting at a desk
with a basket.”

“This one is custom,” Trevor explained. “I built it myself. The curtains
are copper shielded. I’ve got one wire going out. Only for shortwave
transmissions. Otherwise we are completely cut-off from the digital
world.”

Shortwave radio signals were impossible to manipulate with modern
technology. Spies used particular radio transmissions during the cold
war to send and receive signals and communicate around the world. New
technology did not allow for manipulation of the radio waves - physics
did not bend to the digital world.

“We’re safe here,” Trevor assured Reggie. “For now. Not for long.”

“How did you know how to …” Reggie began to ask.

“Find you? Get in touch?” Trevor pulled his jacket from his shoulders
and tossed it on the cot. “Jessica and I communicate via code. Spikes
and blips sent via radio waves and secure messaging channels. I got a
spike on a channel I monitor. With the recent news, I knew I’d get
something from her. Take a seat. We need to download and make backups.”

Trevor motioned toward a chair near a large glass monitor. Reggie obeyed
and felt his shoulders relax as his grip loosened around the bag.

“Let’s see what is so important.” Trevor held out his hand for the bag.
Reggie hesitated before he extended his arms and handed the bag to
Trevor. With the bag in hand, Trevor turned toward another desktop with
a set of glass monitors. A 9mm pistol hung from his belt in a holster at
the small of his back. Reggie had never fired a gun. This thought
triggered in his mind as he feared the sight of it.

Trevor flipped the lid of the laptop open. “Favorite ice cream, right?”

“Huh? Oh, um...yeah. With numbers and symbols instead of…”

“Vowels, yeah, that’s an old standard.”

An old standard? Who the fuck is this guy?, Reggie thought to himself.
The pain reliever was beginning to wear off and his eye socket began to
ache.

Trevor’s fingers tapped the keys and the screen glowed. “okay, we have
to get this whole thing ghosted, then we can examine what was pulled.”

“What about Jessica?” Reggie asked.

“I don’t know where she is.” Trevor confessed. “I expected you would be
her waiting for me at the Metro.”

Reggie’s mind raced with possibilities. She would have taken fifteen
minutes to jog back from the office. Reggie waited a full thirty
minutes. Someone must have got her. Oh God, what if she’s… Reggie could
not finish the thought. He gasped and cupped his hand over his mouth.

“If you’re going to be sick, don’t get any on my machines,” Trevor
pointed toward a wastebasket next to Reggie’s foot. “Use that, please.”

“We have to find her!” Reggie shouted with his hand dropping from his
mouth.

“I know, but we have to protect and analyze what she went after. So if
you’re gonna toss, toss in the basket. We’ll get to her, if we can.”

“Listen, here,” Reggie’s sudden defiance surprised himself. “I’m not
gonna toss and not gonna cool my heels or take a chill pill, whatever is
the phrase of the day. I’ve got to get her back. She could be…”

“Dead?” Trevor turned on his heels and faced Reggie. “Yeah, I know. She
knows. She knew what this assignment might entail. We are not going
anywhere until this information is duplicated and preserved. Then we
have to take care of your eye. Then we can search for her. So shut it.”

Trevor turned back to the laptop and finished his entry of cryptic
computer commands and pressed the enter key. The laptop’s hard disks
whirred with activity and progress bar filled the screen.

Trevor stepped away from the laptop to another desktop as the glass
monitor surged with activity and another progress bar began to fill up.

“Alrighty then, we have a few minutes,” Trevor sucked his teeth, turned
to Reggie. “Let’s take a look at that eye socket.”

###

It was not a painful experience, at least the cleaning of the wound. But
the at-home-surgery after the cleaning made Reggie pass-out. His eye
fluttered open. He laid on the cot. A green army blanket covering him. A
bandage over his wounded eye socket. He could not help but groan as the
dull ache registered in his brain.

“You’re up,” Trevor said cheerfully. “Congratulations, it’s a boy.”

“Very funny,” Reggie said as he felt the cool gauze covering his wound.
“What did you do exactly?”

“Prosthesis implantation,” Trevor said as he turned to his computer
monitors and examined streams of data being processed.

“You’re a fucking doctor, too?” Reggie huffed.

“I have two doctorates actually. But neither of them are in medicine.”
Trevor continued to examine his computer monitors. The data and
information on them processed and rendered at a dizzying rate.

“Did you at least use clean instruments?” Reggie’s concern was
warranted.

“Spit and elbow-grease.”

“Fucking hell, man!” Reggie began to sweat.

“Calm down.” Trevor turned away from his monitors. “What you have there
is grade A piece of the finest hardware not available to even the
highest bidders on a number of very ‘special’ buyers on a very
specialized marketplace.”

“The black market,” Reggie said flatly.

“‘Black’ is so old-fashioned. There are so many more shades of grey that
have wares for sale,” Trevor answered. “You’ve got what I’ve got.”

“Sounds like a disease,” Reggie said.

Trevor pointed his index finger at Reggie, then toward his right eye,
then back at Reggie.

“Some say diseases have a way of making people improve themselves. In
fact, the immune system is a remarkable way of improving the human
condition through sickness. So getting sick is actually evolution,”
Trevor said flatly. “I hope this encourages you.”

“What one of… whatever THAT is? And I thought you weren’t a fucking
Doctor!” Reggie remarked incredulously.

“Yep,” Trevor stood up and walked toward another set of monitors.
“Welcome to the club. Should be ready to power up by now.” He clicked a
few keys on a keyboard and hit enter.

Inside Reggie’s eye socket a hum stirred and he felt gyroscopes and
mechanical tendons and servos trigger. Further in Reggie’s head his mind
began to race and contort with self-doubt.

Jessica. Fuck. Where is all of this right now? What Am I doing? Why
didn’t I just file the fucking papers. Damnit, Jessica, why did you make
me fall for you? Here I am in the conspiracy-SCIF with He-Man here who
can evidently hack the fucking world AND implant shit into people's
heads. I’m gonna get an infection.

Jessica. I miss the last time we made love. I was just perfect. A moment
of weakness at the right time and there was a quiet nook on the stumble
home. Drunk. But aware enough to know it was love and not lust. She
kissed me first. Then a hand explored the front parts of my trousers,
from the inside. Giggles. God, I love the sound of her laughter. That
was the most intoxicating thing about you. I loved making you laugh.
Especially during sex. A beautiful joy between two people holding,
wrestling, and exploring each other. Soft breasts heaving with the
syncopated love making.

You taste like wine. Intoxicating.

Trevor stared blanking at Reggie. Frozen in shock.

“You. Yo...You can hear all of this, can’t you.” Reggie said sheepishly.

“Yeah,” Trevor responded. ”Even got the visualizations in your minds
eye, too. In living color.”

Blank stares between the two men. Shock then a cold panic rushed over
Reggie.

“Well, fuck you! Stay out of my room!” Reggie scoffed.

“Shut the door then!” Trevor answered. “I don’t need to hear your spank
bank recollection. Lock your shit down, soldier.”

“Fuck!” Reggie recoiled on the cot toward the wall. “Fuck! What the
fuck! If you can hear it then THEY CAN HEAR IT!! What did you do! Turn
it off! It will transmit! Is it transmitting!? They’ll blow my fucking
head off, dude!”

“Calm down,” Trevor darted across the room and threw his hands over
Reggie’s flailing arms. He was extremely strong and skipping the gym in
favor of the bottle had not done Reggie any favors in the physical
realm. “The Artificial Intelligence protocols in the implant is still
mapping your brain. Calm down. I’m going to let go, but...you have...to
calm...down.”

Reggie took a breath and shook his head in agreement then he stopped
cold and locked eye to eyes with Trevor.

“Mapping my brain?” Reggie stopped and stared at Trevor. “What the hell
is thing in my skull And WHY is it MAPPING MY FUCKING MIND?!”

“I’ve got the same model installed in my skull,” Trevor held his hands
up palms facing Reggie. “If it was going to blow up, I wouldn’t be
standing here right now. It’s mapping the synapses to figure out how
your mind thinks. What does what and what can it do better. It’s all
part of the A.I. in the software that helps you do so stuff better.”

“‘Do stuff’?” Reggie felt his fingertips press against his thighs.

“Calculations. Strategy. Access wireless networks in close vicinity.
Hack a server farm form the parking lot. Stream porn. Scan the White
House for unsecured comms. You’d be surprised what kinda sexy pics those
staffers trade in thier implants.”

“You mean that thing I lost my eye to that was counting down in my
head?” Reggie asled.

“Yeah, the implant you have now is ten generations ahead of the comms
you guys had. That thing in your head is like a Cray machine, a
supercomputer, at your command. You know what that...”

“I know what a damn Cray machine is.” Reggie attempted to stand. “I’m
not some punk kid, you ass.”

“I wouldn’t…” Trevor said as Reggie stood up then tumbled back into the
cot. “The implant hasn’t had time to to your motor skills and balance.
You’ll need a little longer to boot up.”

“Fuck!” Reggie exclaimed as he tried to shake his legs into working.

“Just give it a few moments. The A.I. is learning you just as much as
you are learning it.” Trevor cautioned. “I can see you’ve got about
fifteen percent left to process.”

“You can see?” Reggie gasped.

“Yeah, I can see it all,” Trevor paused and locked glances with Reggie.
“Don’t worry, you’re too pale for a black guy right now, bro. I’m
running diagnostics and systems checks. You’re almost done.”

“Get. The fuck. Out of...my skull!” Reggie stared daggers at Trevor.

“Just checking on my patient,” Trevor tried to assure him. “I’ve stopped
cataloging your memories. You really stole money from your mom’s purse
to buy comics from a kid on the school bus? Really? That was the first
time you stole? Do you even have those comics any more? Hulk? X-Men?
Spidey?”

GET. OUT. The thought shot form Reggie’s brain to his implant and to
Trevor;s implant and to his brain in nanoseconds.

“Alright,” Trevor spun away form Trevor and back to hius computers. “I’m
done. You’re mapped and should be up and running.”

Reggie felt his legs respond to his thoughts. And he slowly stood up as
he regained full control over his senses.

“Okay, I’m good,” Reggie assured Trevor.

“Take the bandage off,” Trevor commanded. “It will be a little
disorientating for a moment, but then, well, you’ll see.”

Reggie slowly pulled the bandage from his face. The stickiness of the
tape pulled at his moist skin. Light flooded his vision, then everything
came into focus.

“How’s it feel?” Trevor asked.

Reggie saw double, but not like you see after crossing your eyes.
Reggie’s double vision was crystallizing and honing into a
high-definition bitrate in one eye while remaining normal in the other.
The weight in his eye socket was also jarring and awkward.

“It’s like I’ve got a hand-carved billiard ball in my skull.”

“You’ll get used to that,” Trevor assured Reggie. “But wait until you
see the tricks it can do.”

###

Jessica’s eye fluttered open. Rhythmic beeps with bright fluorescent
light surrounded her. Her head was groggy within the drug haze.

“Good to see you’re awake, Ms. Powell,” A voice sounded over an
intercom.

Jessica did not respond. Her eyes focused to the light and she realized
she was strapped to table. But this table was not an ordinary table. It
had straps over the ankles, thighs, waist, and chest. Each of her arms
were strapped to separate wings of the table and extended out
horizontally, in a Christ pose. She was strapped to an execution table.

Her secure-link triggered and activated. A voice inside her skull
sounded, “We can begin now.”

###

Reggie was nervous.

“Just breathe and think,” Trevor encouraged. “It’s connected and making
connections at all times.”

“To what?” Reggie asked.

“Your brain.”

“What the hell?!”

“Just calm down, it’s encrypted and shrouded with a military-grade
firewall on a secure virtual private network.” Otherwise we couldn’t
speak like this.

“Why didn’t your lips move?” Reggie asked.

Because we don’t have to use our mouths. Secure messaging, uses the
vocal patterns from my brain to replicate the sound of my voice
transmitted into your implant. Try it.

“How?”

Stop moving your vocal cords and just think what you want to say.

Fuck. Reggie did not think it worked, then laughter burst from Trevor’s
mouth.

“Really? You want that to be your first word? Technically your third.”

I guess so. An expression of wonder and amazement flooded over Reggie’s
face. Wait. third word? What did I say before?

‘Get. Out.’ It was when I was thumbing through your stolen comic book
memories.

Damn! Reggie mentally exclaimed.

Ain’t it cool? Trevor grinned. We’ve got little time to play. But you’ve
got to learn on the fly. Quick. Fast. And in a hurry.

###

The connection a User shares with his FILTER is symbiotic. If the User
is fresh and rested the FILTER will respond and give the User an
immersive and texture-rich vision of the program and protocols being
utilized. If the User is tired and requires food or rest, the FILTER
will begin to exhibit lapses in its operation. Like a cellphone with a
low battery, the FILTER will stutter and the image overlays and
simulation may not be as crisp.

To the User’s eyes and brain, the FILTER coats the world around him with
layers of information. Everything to the mundane to the hallucinogenic -
to an extent. For instance, a particular game of football, when engaged
with other Users, displays to each User’s eyes and senses football
uniforms, helmets, and cleats layered over other Users physically
surrounding them, but it also will allow for remote Users to engage with
the physical Users. The football is a simulated object visible to the
Users participating in the game and tactile to their senses - some Users
have even said they can smell the leather of the football, but this has
never been fully confirmed through analysis.

To a bystander, not connected through a FILTER, the Users may look like
a group of people playing a make-believe game of football, but to the
Users they are experiencing every tactile and sensory effect of actually
playing a game.

A popular game for Users of the FILTER is a zombie sniper - the
simulated gun in his hands feels like a real rifle. It recoils when
fired. Its weight feels real in his hands. The User shoots at what are
simulated Zombies, but like any simulation it can be manipulated.

Representative Baker was just one of these simulations overlayed on to
the real world. OpOne designed this protocol himself. It was useful for
particular missions requiring stealth and divorce from any possible
blowback. What would a police detective say to a person saying they did
not know the gun in their hands was real? That they thought they were
playing a game. Vision Systems could easily spin any potential
investigation with a rendering of what a User really sees versus what is
actually occurring.

OpOne studied the JFK Assassination for the basis of this particular
protocol. How else can you create the perfect Patsy? And it did. The
perfect assassin does not know he is killing, nor why he is killing. Not
being able to conjure up a reason for the conspiracy is a simple way to
divorce any suspicion from the act and possible motives. Killing the
Patsy would not have been necessary and there would be no need for any
Jack Rubys in the equation.

Years ago OpOne pitched this application to Clemens and Robinson. They
were skeptical about the possibility for blowback and implication of
Vision Systems and ultimately the U.S. Government. To quell their fears,
OpOne designed a failsafe function to wipe the FILTER from the User.
They agreed to a test-run, somewhere far away from the U.S. mainland.
OpOne pulled up an old report of one of his first test subjects and
began reliving the experience in his Secure-link.

OLD TOWN SQUARE, PRAGUE, CZECH REPUBLIC - Operator Alpha Zero-two.

Perched in a window of an art museum overlooking the Old Town Square, a
User, as instructed by his FILTER Mission was to pay admission to enter
the museum. Walk through the exhibit and find an open window on the
third floor. He did so.

Once at the window, his FILTER displayed instructions to his heads up
display.

Your targets are the infected. They are found in the square. You must
neutralize them before infection spreads.

The User clicked acknowledgment of the mission and began to feel a
sniper rifle materialize in his hands. He raised the rifle to his
shoulder and peered through the scope. Through his FILTER, the infected
glowed green, the uninfected a pale blue.

The User was able to get off six shots before the massive city square
emptied. Six shots - six kills. The game was a success. The User enjoyed
the elation of the immersive experience. The rifle in his hands
deconstructed and slipped back into the FILTER system that had bonded
with him just that morning. He calmly walked out of the museum into the
hail of police gunfire.

The FILTER system’s failsafe triggered at the last heartbeat of the User
and the nanotechnology devoured itself into base elements and dust -
resembling cocaine or some other powdered drug to the naked eye. The
User would be considered some lone wolf madman blasted out of his mind
on some drug. Fifteen minutes of news and it would be dropped as “just
another isolated incident.”

This mission was a success. And further testing was conducted.
Refinements were made. Clemens and Robinson did not like the use of a
projectile. OpOne struggled with this problem for months until he found
a sonic bullet project from the Defense Advanced Research Projects
Agency. DARPA had been the repository for all the seemingly impossible
projects that the military industrial complex wanted and funded to a
nauseating amount.

The sonic bullet, in the form OpOne discovered, was a crude theory of
using a pulse of air to pierce the soft tissue of the target. Per the
records, it was only able to create a bruise, perhaps break a small
bone. It was defunded and the research was archived.

From those ashes OpOne took the basic concepts and with a few
simulations and application of the quantum computer, he had at his
disposal a Vision System able to mix sound and a laser for a body
penetrating “bullet” that was far more effective. The flux of sound and
direction of a laser in any number of applications is constantly used in
various health sciences OpOne just figured out how to use it to kill a
person. It was the perfect weapon - silent and undetectable and wielded
by a person unsuspecting of its potency; a baby with a loaded gun.

Intermission:

‘‘SEC. 702. PROCEDURES FOR TARGETING CERTAIN PERSONS OUTSIDE

THE UNITED STATES OTHER THAN UNITED STATES PERSONS.

‘‘(a)...

(i)(3) ...the Director of National Intelligence may authorize...,the
targeting of persons reasonably believed to be located outside the
United States to acquire foreign intelligence information.

‘‘(b) LIMITATIONS…(1) may not intentionally target any person known at
the time of acquisition to be located in the United States;

‘‘(2) may not intentionally target a person reasonably
