decks looming over the Operator station flickered. The climate controls
clicked on and off randomly. Access pads flashed red-green-red-green -
stuck in a chaotic cycle.

“We’re under attack,” Robinson’s knuckles whitened under his dark skin.
“Fucking hackers and terrorists.”

This was not an attack that you see in movies. It was not artillery
being fired in from a ship in the channel. It was not the taking of
Omaha beach. No bullets zipping by soldier ears. No gore and carnage
blasting up from the sand. It was a digital attack. Focused on
motherboards and server farms, while overheating fiber-optic wiring with
repeated commands meant to drive up CPU cycles to melt the wiring render
the machines inert and useless, effectively destroying hundreds of
millions of dollars in computer networks and data. This was in every
aspect the biggest and most comprehensive denial of service attack ever
attempted. A denial of service - or DDOS - attack attempts to overload
the capacity of a computer several or network by randomly sending
requests and and information. The capacity of the network and ability of
the servers to process the information and renders the machines
unresponsive and useless for the education for the attack.

“We can’t isolate the sources of these attacks. It’s everything in the
book. Denial of Service attacks. IP pings. Power grid fluctuations. It’s
everything and all things in and out of the networks.” An Operator
shouted toward Robinson from the floor of the Operator’s room.

In his ears, Robinson heard a muffled and shouted version of the
Operator; one from his secure-link and the other through the glass.

Robinson was a pillar of stone, but his mind raced with probabilities
and strategies. It’s every angle. It’s every option in an out. They are
throwing every play in the book at us with infinite players. It’s a new
steel. A game changer. His mind flashed to an old history lesson from
college. A new kind of steel emerged in Scandinavia. Forged at higher
temperatures and creating a steel that dominated the battlefield until
the Industrial Revolution. Ulfberht swords. Named for the inscription
along the blade. It was branded there and made a daunting impression on
the adversaries of its holder.

Another lesson streaked across Robinson’s mind, amid the chaos
surrounding him. When faced with an insurmountable force or out matched
weapon, it is often the best option to withdraw and vanish.

“Operator,” Robinson calmly spoke to his chief Operator whose eyes were
wild with anxiety. “Shut it down. Take it offline. All of it.”

The Operator stopped dead in his tracks and shot his eyes toward
Robinson from the Operator’s Room.

“That’s an order, Operator,” Robinson eased his stance, relaxed his
shoulders, and cupped his hands together behind his back - in an at-ease
military stance.

The Operator charged out of the Operator’s Room and into the hallway. He
broke into a run toward the central elevator axis. Once there, he
pressed the down button repeatedly. The elevator door opened and then
shut, then opened halfway and shut again. He pressed through the
malfunctioning elevator doors and slammed through the doors to the the
emergency stairs. He descended down toward the server farms and into a
secured room because of the hacks the electronic door was locking and
unlocking rapidly. Timing a shoulder push in-between the locks locking
and unlocking the operator found his way to the center of the servers
farm room with it's long aisles of tall cabinet-sized servers.

He yanked the door opened hard to find a terrified security guard with
his sidearm shaking in his right hand.

With his hands up, the Operator shouted, “Don’t shoot! Orders from
Command!”

Suddenly, a shrieking signal shot through their secure-links. They are
in the secure-links. Fuck!

The security guard dropped his sidearm to the floor, grabbed his head
and blood began to trickle from his nose.

Despite the shrieking pain in his head, The Operator rushed toward a
long tube glowing blue with heat and electricity. It was three feet in
diameter. The backbone of the globally connected world. It was the
doorway from and to everywhere Vision Systems’ networks reached. If it
could not be fail-safed it had to be cut - physically.

A hand scanner blinked green-red-green-red next to the control panel for
the glowing tube. The monitor blinked and displayed garbled text and
pornographic images.

Another jolt of the mind-wrenching signal shot through his secure-link.
No other option.

The Operator grasped a lever alongside the glowing tube, bit his lip,
and yanked hard.

Two rings of explosives around both ends of the tube fired off in series
of quick small and controlled explosions. The glow ceased with sparks
and fizzles.

Immeadiately, half the world went dark.

Street lights stopped, flashed caution and reset.

Trains sputtered on their tracks and then resumed the travel.

Radar screens of airports flickered - switched off like old televisions
and snapped back on.

Power grids flickered and rebooted.

For a brief moment, the world was deathly quiet.

Blood dripped from the Operator’s nose and tapped his shoe. The smell of
sulfur and copper hung in the air - like a ghost.

For a brief moment, the Operator’s mind was quiet and still. There was
no buzzing message in the corner of his mind. His heart beat in his
chest. He felt the pressure of his weight on the pads of his feet inside
his shoes. The weight of his arms pulling on his shoulders. It was a
brief reprieve from the electric pulse of his world.

Vision System’s computers and internal networks began to come back
online. The internal communications triggered and his secure-link
flooded with messages from other Operators and Robinson on the isolated
internal networks.

Tears began to roll down his cheeks as the deafness of disconnection
left his senses.

###

The electronic stock boards flickered and sputtered as the quotes
displayed in garbled and strange symbols. Cryptic lettering replaced
numerals. The floor traders froze paused from their normal frenzy of
buying and selling based on the rise and tumble of the boards quotes and
trends.

Suddenly the lights blacked out and red emergency flood lights
illuminated the exits.

A few shrieks escaped weak lips and throats. But the glow of handheld
datapads allowed the traders to see in the dark.

What was truly terrifying was the data. It was stopped. Frozen. Broken.
In the stock market, data is money. Without it, there is no wealth.

The servers froze. Halted. A virus inserted in a single trade infected
the backbone of the stock market. It was successful. In an instant,
trillions of dollars in wealth disappeared.

The datapads in the hands of the floor traders were little more than
overpriced flashlights.

Panic flooded the stock building. Filling it to the brim,sucking the
oxygen out of the room like a vacuum.

The panic shot out of the room through the news cameras filming their
midday market reports.

The attack was perfectly timed. It's effect was maximized with every
breath taken while surrounded by darkness. The world shrugged and
sputtered.

Just as the news anchors caught their bearing on what was occurring.
Their producers shouted into their earpieces about the effects of the
blackout and rumors of the virus attacking the world’s leading stock
markets.

The reports of the network crash and the tremendous loss of wealth
flashed across almost every television and computer screen in the world.

For fifteen minutes, the reporters and anchors repeated the keywords fed
into their ears by the little clear wires snaking up from their shirt
collars.

After ten minutes, reports of bodies falling from skyscrapers in
Manhattan began to spark on social media. Pictures followed shortly
thereafter. Bloody pools surrounding crumped shattered shells of flesh,
bone, and meat.

Gunshots sounded in normally quiet upscale neighborhoods tucked into the
most expensive zip codes of the United States. Scenes of desperation
fueled by blind reaction awaited the next unfortunate person to enter
those rooms.

Fires burst out of car windows as glass store fronts shattered from the
impact of bricks thrown through them. The goods lining the shelves were
soon missing and likely found for a severely reduced sale price on a
darker market.

Less than 144 lines of computer code inserted through a routine stock
trade succeeded in setting the world on fire.

Then, the lights came back on in the trading room. The servers roared
back to life. The wealth - thought lost - filled the digital account
books across the world.

The "glitch" was widely reported. It was blamed for the widespread panic
that covered the world. The "glitch" was blamed for the suicides,
rioting, and looting. It was not a glitch.

In a bunker, tucked behind yards of concrete and steel, a series of
monitors filled with code and signal quietly watched the impact of the
virus. The effects catalogued and indexed for an after-operation report.
One monitor did not change, but it's code remained stagnant. This code
was the virus. This room was the launchpad from which this disaster
sprang.

"The after report should take about an hour to compile," an Operator
leaning back into his chair said into his headset.

"Confirmed," a voice answered in his ear. "Continue monitoring and
supplement as needed."

Robinson blinked away the communications link. He called up a national
security transmittal he had prepared prior to launching this operation.
It was filled with responses meant to paint hackers and terrorists
responsible for the attack on the stock market. It was well received
because it was easy to accept. The news networks repeated, nearly
verbatim.

Soon the world was terrified of the black-faced hackers and bloodthirsty
terrorists that caused the stock market to crash, and were to blame for
all the horribles that followed.

The outcry was deafening across every avenue of expression available.
Congress had banded together on the steps of the Capitol earlier that
afternoon and sung the National Anthem. At nine that evening, the
President issued an executive order from the Oval Office while sitting
at his desk. The country's highest elected official stared into the
television camera with his graying hair and tired, but steady eyes and
wiped away every constitutional right held by every person under the
protection of the Constitution.

He issued an executive order declaring martial law within 50 miles of
every national security facility under control of the United States.
This effectively opened an umbrella over the heads of virtually every
corner of the world.

His calm voice washed over the world. The vast majority of the hearts
and minds of his people felt reassured. They felt calm at the end of a
truly violent and horrible day. He did not promise one hundred
protection, he only promised it would not happen again under his watch.

The world thanked him as tired eyes eased into restful sleep.

They did not know their lives were no longer theirs. Their homes no
longer secure against intrusion. Their bank accounts no longer respected
as private. Their minds and thoughts no longer their own. Virtually
every particulate of their lives were now opened fresh and unprotected
from the unblinking stare of what was no longer their government but
rather their worst enemy.

The world awoke the next morning to tanks at street corners and the
freedoms it once held became like sand in the grip of a clenched fist.

20 - Feigned Outrage

  “...unlawful obstructions, combinations, or assemblages, or rebellion
  against the authority of the United States, make it impracticable to
  enforce the laws of the United States in any State by the ordinary
  course of judicial proceedings, he may call into Federal service such
  of the militia of any State, and use such of the armed forces, as he
  considers necessary to enforce those laws or to suppress the
  rebellion.”

  10 US Code 252, Use of militia and armed forces to enfroce Federal
  authroity.

###

Senator Ammerstand had not left his office since the President's decree.
He and his staff had been working around the clock and sleeping in
shifts. They had been researching and fact-checking the documents and
reports in the press and released by the mysterious whistleblower.

The offices were covered with paper and filled with the roar of
typewriters. Mountains of legal pads littered the desks along with empty
cups of coffee.

A packet of documents had been delivered to Senator Ammerstand late last
night. With a clear note handed written on the outside: “Organic Eyes
Only - No Secure-link.”

The documents detailed a number of classified operations involving
Vision Systems. Operation reports filled with details that turned the
Senator’s blood cold.

The Senator was beginning to realize all the years of classified
briefings were half-truths filled with omissions. The information was
true but the application was never fleshed out. A dark world unfolded
before him in the reports with tentacles wrapped around the throats of
nearly every person, corporation, and government entity around the
globe.

"What did they do wrong?" The Senator muttered at his desk. His aide
paused from his note taking. It had been three days since martial law
had been ordered. "Tim? What did they do wrong? What rule did they
break?"

The Senator paused and looked at his befuddled aide. His eyes bloodshot.
His collar unbuttoned; tie loosened.

"The law." Tim's brain was not wired to work on less than four hours
sleep taken on an army surplus cot in a supply room.

"It's not broken if the law allowed the conduct," the Senator a lifted a
copy of the United States Code from his desk and tossed it to an empty
space between him and Tim, landing with a heavy thud.

"Was it wrong? Yes. Was it allowed? Yes. We allowed the conduct. Gave
them carte blanche to train every available intelligence weapon against
the citizenry and persons they swore an oath to protect, all in the
guise of protecting those from themselves. They even put a goddamn bug
in the brains and eyes of Congress." The Senator pointed to his own
right eye.

The morning the newspapers reported the surveillance leaks, the Senator
had his staffs' Secure-links disabled by their tech officer. Then the
computers were unplugged and typewriters were brought up from storage.
Only a handful of computers were allowed to remain on and only one
connected to the Internet; all had any web camera lens blocked with an
opaque post-it note. A small sneaker-network sprang up in the Senator’s
office and while many of his Staffers and Aides agonized over the
technological reversion in the office, but from the information they
read, studied, and researched their blood ran cold and stomachs twist as
they realized their own eyes had been made to spy on their every move.

"It shall be the right of the people to be secure in their person,
places, papers, and effects," the Senator said. "And no warrants shall
issue, but upon probable cause." He finished the half quote of the
Fourth Amendment. Tim nodded and noticed his desk copy of the
Declaration of Independence and Constitution teetering on the edge of
his desk.

"The Founding Fathers thought this was important. They put it forth on
their list of things to keep sacred," the Senator stood from his desk
chair and straightened his tie.

The roar of work in the senator’s office dulled as a hush settled as the
Senator rushed out into the hallway toward the exit of the building.

"Wait! Senator!" Tim chased after Senator Ammerstand. "They may arrest
you!"

Outside the Senate office building an army tank sat in the intersection
of First Street and Constitution Avenue. Soldiers with assault rifles
stood at the ready circled around the tank. Fifty feet from the steps of
the Senate Office building a corral was set up and held reporters with
their cameras.

Senator Ammerstand burst out of the double doors of the building. The
soldiers startled to attention. The audible sound of safeties clicking
off was heard. A gasp registered from the throats of some of the press.
Cameras powered up, focused on the gated Senator, and began to record.

The Senator approached the soldiers. His shoes tapping on the concrete
in the stillness of the afternoon.

"Good afternoon, Gentlemen. Where y'all from?" The Senator stopped
twenty feet from the soldiers surrounding the tank.

"Ohio," a lone voice answered.

"Cincy or Cleveland?" the Senator asked.

"Go Browns!" Another voice chimed in and a chuckle eased over the
soldiers.

"Can we talk candidly, Gentlemen?" The Senator unbuttoned his suit
jacket and tucked a hand into his pant pocket while gesturing with his
free hand. "I understand why you are here. I understand the orders you
are following. I served.” He tapped a lapel pin with the Marine Corps
Eagle, Globe, and Anchor with his free hand over his heart and just
below his Senate lapel pin. “I know the oath you took. I took a similar
oath. And added onto that one with the one I took in honor of my current
position. Not to mention to serve and protect our common employer."

He paused and gestured toward the television reporters and cameras.

"Now, I understand we’re under orders right now. You have your’s from
your Commanding Officer, who got them from his Commanding Officer, and
so on up the chain. I have my orders too.”

Senator Ammerstand softened the glare he shot from his eyes and eased a
kind smile across his face.

“I'm going to ask you to ignore those orders for the next twenty
minutes. Will you do that for me, not as a Senator, not as a Marine, not
even as a Patriot, but as a fellow citizen?"

Silence shrouded the group.

"Gentlemen, I will take that as a ‘yes,’"

The Senator turned from the soldiers and approached the corral of
reporters.

Shouts of questions began to pepper the Senator. He ignored them, raised
a hand to quiet the shouts, and pulled open part of the corral allowing
the reporters to move about freely.

"Please follow me," the Senator asked the reporters.

He crossed in front of the soldiers. "Gentlemen, would you also join us?
I’m not giving you any orders. I don’t hold that power. I’m just asking
for company. Please walk with me."

The Senator walked toward the east face of the Capitol. His aides and
staff had crowded at the entrance of the office building.

A corporal flanked the Senator to his left. A reporter from the Bulletin
paced them on the Senator’s right side.

"Son, what is your name?" the Senator asked as he walked toward the
Capitol.

"Jackson, Vance Jackson," the soldier answered. The reporter was
furiously scribbling notes as he kept pace. "My friends and family call
me Van."

"May I call you Van?" the Senator asked.

"Yes, sir."

"Please call me, Chuck."

Van nodded affirmingly and smiled.

They continued until the Capitol was less than 75 yards in front of the
Senator. It was a good backdrop for the cameras. The sun was low in the
sky and the golden hour was just beginning. The marble steps of the
United States Capitol raised the tall Senator above the crowd. He
stopped his advance and spun around. To one side the reporters had
followed. To the other flank the soldiers had followed.

The Senator paused and smiled.

"I speak to you today not as a Senator..."

The broadcast carried over the news networks. The whole march had been
picked up as it proceeded. In direct violation of the order imposing
martial law issued three days ago the national news networks were
broadcasting. It was currently being watched by more than ten million
viewers; rebroadcast on both the Internet and via Virtual Private
Networks and recast on countless websites and media platforms. .

"Our country is at a crossroads. We are facing an unprecedented
challenge that we cannot ignore. I am not here to lay blame at the
threshold of the Whitehouse. Nor am I here to accept blame on behalf of
the Senate and the House. All are equally to blame for the state of this
great nation at this moment."

He paused, Senator Ammerstand was previously not only a Corporal in the
Marine Corps, but also a trial lawyer and understood the importance of
timing when giving an argument.

"As your leaders we are shouldered with the burden and honor of
safeguarding the rights and liberties of persons protected by the
Constitution. We have failed in that responsibility. This is a dreadful
failure in the course of my service, under my command, and the blame
falling squarely on my shoulders."

"The protection of this republic is one that is a great burden, worthy
of only the most honorable of our neighbors. Corporal Vance Jackson is
one of those honorable men. He and I shared a walk this afternoon and
I'm proud to consider him a friend."

The Corporal nervously smiled as the Senator patted him on the back.

"But that's not why I'm here today, nor is it the reason he is here
today. We stand here this afternoon because of an order issued through
the office of the President of the United States. To that order, I, nor
he, challenged the authenticity of the order or the veracity of the
threats to our great nation which warranted the extreme action buried
within that order. But we should challenge the need of this drastic
measure when there is compelling evidence that we have hobbled this
nation by our own design."

Camera shutters whirred with the pause of the Senator’s speech.

"We have allowed one of the sentinels of this nation too much slack on
its leash and it has encircled us. Every person hearing my words today
is subject to the unlawful search and seizure of their person, papers,
places, and effects. This long honored principle has been embedded in
the very DNA of our country since its inception. 'No warrants shall
issue, but upon probable cause.' Frankly, we have tossed away even the
need for warrants. We scrubbed away those words when we as your leaders
ignored the bill of rights and offered the vile of perceived safety to
you, the public. We passed the operative acts and laws allowing for this
intrusion of your privacy whole cloth and without question. We promised
to safeguard the Constitution when we accepted the duties of our
service. For that I apologize. For that dereliction of duty, I am
ashamed. For the willful ignorance of the common mission we all share in
providing for and sheltering the inalienable rights given by our
creators, I have failed you, my fellow Americans."

He paused and turned to the Corporal to his right.

"Van? I owe you an apology and ask forgiveness." The Senator’s right
hand lowered from the Corporal’s shoulder and opened seeking redemption.
The Corporal's right hand loosened from the grip of his rifle and took
the Senator's hand.

The Senator’s eyes focused on the faces that followed down the line
behind the Corporals' and seemed to request forgiveness from each
soldier. His gaze stopped when he locked onto the lenses of the video
cameras.

"Finally, I ask my fellow Americans for their forgiveness. I refuse to
continue not serving my fellow Americans as I have over the past 72
hours. I ask that my fellow Congressmen and Congresswomen join me in the
immediate repeal the current order imposing martial law and not stop
until we root out the terrible violations of our Constitution that have
occurred thus far. Thank you."

The Corporal's hand slipped from the Senator's as he spun on his heels
and walked toward the Capitol steps. He slipped between a barricade and
past more soldiers and tactical police. He proceeded up the eastern
steps of the Capitol and entered the building.

The news networks carried every minute of the impromptu news conference
and apology of the Senator.

Tim waited for the Senator at the entrance to the underground subway
between the Capitol and the Senate office buildings.

The Senator was visibly pale and his collar was unbuttoned.

"I can't believe you did that, Sir!" Tim was dumbfounded.

"It had to be done. You break up a log jam with a stick and pressure."

"How did you know that would work?" Tim ushered the Senator on to the
automated subway car.

"I didn't. I just hoped everyone still remembered they were American."

The subway car clicked and trundled through the tunnel toward the Senate
office building.

"Was it only for show?" The question shot out of Tim's mouth.

"No, that was the easy part. Now we have to marshal our friends and
enemies to do the right thing."

###

Life slowly returned to Washington, D.C. For three days tanks and
soldiers blocked every avenue of ingress and egress throughout the city.
The Army and National Guard receded back to their bases. Slowly the news
reported everyday online and broadcast that the world was getting back
to normal.

Robinson tapped his fingers on the tabletop as the coffee steamed in a
cup before him.

The meeting was set through unconventional channels. Senator Ammerstand
was a half hour late, on purpose.

A folder lay on the table with a seal marked "Top Secret."

Ammerstand crossed the windows and entered the front door of the diner.
It was known as the Silver Dollar. Known for greasy patty melts and
crispy hash browns for nearly forty years. Posters of classic movies
lined the walls.

Robinson didn't acknowledge the Senator but continued to stare out the
windows.

"General? I trust you haven't ordered yet?" Ammerstand tried to make
small talk.

"Just coffee, Senator," Robinson patted the folder. "I asked to speak
with you because I wanted to let you know about a little information
that I discovered. I wanted to make you aware it was out there in case
it is revealed to harm your efforts."

"You suck at blackmail."

Robinson scoffed as he sipped his coffee. "Why do you say that?"

"Because I know my life, General. I know what your prop there, has on
me. Nice effect with the seal, by the way. Looks good and official."

"So you don't want to see it?" Robinson growled.

"I know what it says. I know that my wife had an abortion while we were
in college together. We were kids, we weren't ready to have children.
She knows I slept with my best friend’s girlfriend during a time that my
wife and I were separated. It was a moment of weakness. I've discussed
this with my wife, we went to counseling. We have promised ourselves to
each other and renewed our lives together. Oh, I smoked a lot of pot in
high school. I mean A LOT! Even owned a VHS of a Cheech and CHong movie.
Wore that thing out, I smoked and watched it so much. Anything else?"

Robinson slashed the seal on the folder open with a butter knife and
opened the folder.

"What about Timothy Rogers?" Robinson asked.

"He's my personal aide and has worked for me since I first practiced
law. I'm sure he has secrets. Those are his own. Those are what makes
him human. I've alerted my whole staff to clean their closets and go to
confession. And yes, I know Tim's gay. I also know he was dishonorably
discharged from the Air Force for striking an officer. What's missing
from your little folder there is the officer was his lover and the fight
was about whether to re-up their commissions together and at the same
base.

"But all of that did not and has not impeded his ability to work in my
law office and he has not failed in his duties during the course of his
service to this country while I have been a Senator.

"He's also very good with kids. My kids call him, Uncle Timmy."

Robinson closed his folder and took a sip from his coffee. His stare was
cold.

"We have a great opportunity for us to bury the hatchet," Robinson said.

"That's where the problem lies, General." Ammerstand shifted in his seat
as he adjusted his jacket. "There should have never been a hatchet. We
are supposed to work together, but you wanted something more. That pesky
bill of rights just got in the way. So, with a little smoke and fire
along with some clever orchestrations of actual terrorism you supplanted
the very string of rights and protections right out from under all of
us. Hell, we helped you wrap the body in towels and rugs as you dragged
it out the back door."

"Senator, the wild language isn't needed," Robinson waived off the
waitress from refilling his coffee and to keep their conversation quiet.

"Don't you dare tell me what is and isn't needed. I'm telling you,
General. You've got three days to resign. Three. I'm not going to unpack
a folder of your personal life in front of a committee hearing or on the
news, web, or telegraph!"

Ammerstand realized his voice was rising and reset his tone.

"I don't care what you do, but it will never be in relation to a
government operation for surveillance, data, National Security. You'll
retire. You'll be comfortable. Learn fly fishing. Go to your cabin in
Michigan. You haven't been there in a while."

Robinson's eyebrow raised, he did not expect Ammerstand to know about
his cabin.

"You've served, General. You've served your time and you'll be honored
by the public. We don't want a bloody coup. We don't want legacy to be
tarnished any further. The public doesn't need that and we need to move
forward. But we need to remember the mistakes. There won't be blame."

Robinson motioned for the waitress. Her footsteps tapped on the linoleum
floor.

"Miss, thank you, but my friend and I have to cut our brunch short
today. What do I owe you for the coffee?"

"Not a problem, Sir. Here's your check," she ripped it from her pad and
placed it on the table.

Her footsteps retreated.

"I suppose to make sure this couldn't be considered a bribe, I should
pay for my own coffee," Robinson pulled his wallet out and flipped a
five dollar bill on to the check. "Senator? I wish you a good day."

Ammerstand sighed then sucked his teeth.

Robinson stood and whipped his jacket around his shoulders.

"Oh and Senator, so that there is no misunderstanding. I will not go
quietly into the night. And you should reconsider your stock portfolio,"
Robinson leaned closer to Ammerstand's ear. "It's not just the true lies
we know about, but also the lies we create, command, and control."

Robinson stood up straight. "Give my best to your wife. She is looking
better since rehab."

###

Senator Burchart was sitting down when his secure-link chimed with a
download link from Robinson. Or who he thought was Robinson.

It began with a relatively friendly note.

Dear Senator, we hope this finds you well. We also hope that this may
help you fully comprehend the terror ordinary people feel everyday.
Sincerely, the other side of the pillow.

The documents fluttered into Burchart’s secure-link. A parade of
horribles unfolded.

A note at the end was a video file with a digitally scrambled voice. It
auto-played.

Senator, thank you for attending this episode of "This is Your Shit." We
hope it has been enlightening. We understand that your are not the
devil. We understand you are human, as are we. We trust you are thinking
right now. This information was not cobbled together by us. But rather
it was taken from a close friend of yours. It was part of a poison pill
program that would have released to the public upon a number of
different events.

The video paused.

We trust these events will occur so we have taken the liberty to replace
this payload with something less damaging. We are not asking for favors,
we are asking for your re-dedication to the oath you took when you chose
public service and became a Senator. We trust you to do the right thing.
We trust you will still be able to recognize the right thing. This
package has been backed up just in case you struggle with your morality.

The video shuddered and reset. The shape of the figure changed and the
voice pitch returned higher.

Senator, you are now in possession of an additional selection of
documents.

A document package pinged in Burchart’s secure-link.

This was cobbled together from black files and countermeasure protocols
that monitored your office and committees. There are numerous emails and
communications. But more importantly, there are drafts of a special
report of intelligence oversight and one investigation regarding
interrogation tactics engaged in by black-ops under the direction of the
CIA. This was a set of documents that was of particular interest to
General Robinson. Also included are transmissions and download logs from
when he allowed other arms of the intelligence community to access your
networks and computers.

We trust you will recognize the importance of these documents and the
moral conundrum it places your office in. We trust you will make the
right choice, Senator Burchart.

The video clicked off and the documents unfolded in Burchart’s heads up
display. The dirty laundry of his whole life polluted his vision.

The choice was difficult, but not difficult to make.

21 - Contact

Jessica had not heard from either Reggie or Trevor in days. But she had
seen the world melting down in the news. The stock market hacks were of
particular interest. The media continued to blame the nefarious elements
in the digital underworld.

"It's easy to blame the mute," Jessica said as she curled up on a small
couch watching the news from the Pirate Political Party's Headquarters
in Iceland.

The news droned further with talking heads metaphorically attempting to
pour oil over the barricades against the barbarians at the gate.

A ping alerted in Jessica's secure-link. Nobody should be able to ping
her. She had reprogrammed her link.

Again, the ping triggered behind her eye.

It couldn't be Reggie. Trevor would know better than to try in this
form.

Ping.

She returned the ping.

A request for a comm-link opened. She opened a link after bouncing the
connection through a number of firewalls and across a handful of
continents.

"That wasn't necessary," the voice on the other end of the connection
said.

"I'll be the judge of that," Jessica answered.

"I pinged you. I knew where you were before you knew who pinged you."

"Okay, you've made your point," Jessica sighed, "What do you want?"

"I'm hoping to help you out. Reggie and I have talked and I think you
are in the best position to release the information we want out there."

"How about all of it?" Jessica said. "I don't think any of this needs to
be be kept from the world."

"I agree," said the voice. "But some information resembles drops of
water and some are nuclear bombs."

"I think the bombs need to be handled delicately," Jessica answered.
"The water should flow, regardless."

"I want to help," the voice said.

"Think of this as a job interview," Jessica said. "Let's begin to
interview."

"You're watching the international broadcast feed of World News Network.
The room you are sitting in has four wired-appliances. The blanket you
have pulled over your lap is the one thing you allowed yourself to bring
from Washington when you went underground and left the country. You had
it in your townhouse. It was hardly used and usually hung on the back of
your couch."

"Stop it," Jessica hissed. "You've made your point. You're an insider.
You've got access to some good hardware and skills to use it. I bet you
have more information that you would like to release. More than what we
know about right now."

A pause crept between them.

"I don't know," the voice announced. "I don't know if I want to get into
bed with you."

"We haven't even had dinner yet," Jessica replied.

"Drinks neither," the voice chuckled.

"Listen," Jessica threw the blanket off her lap and leaned forward. "I
know you’re watching right now. I know I'm a target. My face has been on
the fucking news. Yours hasn't. You're still anonymous and hungry to get
this shit off your chest. Out of your mind. It's a relief. Trust me. I
was tired of keeping it bottled up in my brain."

"I built it," the voice sighed. "God. That is a relief just to admit to
someone."

Jessica's skin and blood flashed cold, as if an icy wind suddenly
engulfed her.

"What do you mean?" Jessica asked.

"I built it. Designed it. The whole system. Everything from the back
doors to the front doors. The piggy-backing into wired components and
webcams, all of it. To tell you the truth I was just looking for a new
perspective when I was studying technology. It wasn't easy, but it was
fun. It was just the beginning."

Jessica realized this wasn't just a hacker looking to piss on big
brother. He was big brother.

"I didn't intend all of this," the voice cracked. "I was just taking my
theories and designs to their logical levels and fleshing them out. I
didn't think I'd get into every home in the world and peek into every
pocket of every person."

"It's okay," Jessica said.

"See, that's just it. I don't think it is okay," the voice said. "It
shouldn't be okay. Oppenheimer said it best after the first successful
test of the nuclear bomb, '...now I have become Death, the destroyer of
worlds.'"

"It's not the end," Jessica tried to comfort him. "There is always a new
angle. A new beginning."

"There is no putting this genie back in the bottle, Jessica."

He used her real name, causing chills down her spine.

"It won't stop unless we rewind time."

"Stopping it doesn't need a time machine," Jessica cautioned.

"No, it's like giving a bottle to an alcoholic. They won't just not
drink it. They'll use it and slurp it down. The headache the next
morning will just be the price of victory...over the bottle the night
before."

"So how do we do it?" Jessica dared to push the conversation along.

"They don't just give it up," the voice said. "They won't. They can't.
You keep a heroin addict from injecting themselves by breaking their
arms. The cast keeps them from touching the vein. That's the only way."

"You make it sound so final and desperate." The tone in his tone was
beginning to scare Jessica.

"It is, because it is. I've lived this as my life," the voice said.
"Jessica, I'm sending you some files. Let them lose. They'll catch on
like wildfire. I'll begin to dismantle and limit from my end, but not
before Rome burns."

The connection clicked off. Files filled Jessica's secure-link.

###

The message was short but clear.

These were dropped from inside. Told to distribute...widespread.

22 - Fear Equals Control

Like a pauper begging a rich man, credibility followed the report as it
leaked on the Central Intelligence Agency’s own letterhead: written in
their own words, from their perspective, and by their own direction. All
marked with “Top Secret, Eyes Only, National Command Level 3” in the
header of each page.

The 200 hundred page report contained admissions and violations of
almost every aspect of the U.S. Constitution. Therein the CIA admitted
to hacking the computers and secure networks of Senate and House
committees charged with oversight of US intelligence operations. In
short, the watchers watched the watchers while watching themselves.

Some media commentators described the intrusion as being on a scale
that, “the watchers could have watched the watchers take a shit.”

It was the biggest news story of the week and posted on every news
outlet and blog across the country and the world. Comment forums flooded
with everything from outrage to cautious denial and meager defenses of
the internal spying.

Jessica recorded a statement and posted to a video sharing website;
almost every newspaper and channel in the world picked it up and ran it
on repeat. She released it without concealing her face or voice. She
hoped it would take the heat off of Reggie.

###

Implement Project Oklahoma.

###

This is not a drill. Your objective is to attack and disable the power
substation. Intelligence reports have placed this substation deep in
enemy-controlled territory. We suspect there are numerous terrorist
cells operating in close proximity to the substation. Destroying their
power supply will disrupt their current missions. Deadly force
authorized. Shoot suspected terrorists on sight.

The briefing instructions flashed and scrolled across their
Heads-Up-Displays. A team of 12 Users rode in the back of a blacked out
passenger van. Their heads and shoulders rocking back and forth with the
motion of the van. The click and clatter of checking their weapons along
with the dull rumble of the engine the only soundtrack.

The van trundled to a stop.

Their HUDs flashed. Execute the mission.

The doors of the van flew open. The Users poured out with their weapons
at the ready. Safety’s clicked off and laser sights traced the outlines
and doorways of the buildings on the street. It was a normal American
street - to normal eyes.

The Users saw a graffiti tagged and desolate run down husk of a city
landscape. Urban decay had set in and patriotic bile began to burn the
throats of the Users. They hated the cancer they fought against - the
unAmerican Snowflakes.

These feelings were easy to embed into the User’s thoughts. It was all
part of the program - the mission. It made the success probabilities
rise dramatically. When you hold a hammer; all you see are nails.
Propaganda works very well in that environment.

Set the charges.

A User set a small C4 charge on the chained lock of the gate surrounding
the substation.

Fire in …3...2...1.

The gate burst open with a flash and smoke.

The Users entered the opened gate and splintered off surrounding the
substation.

The small explosion awoke Henry Calper. A 57-year-old plumber with an
ex-wife and two little girls. Joint custody. He planned on taking his
daughters to a local carnival this weekend. His thoughts when he heard
the blast were not of his daughters, but worried it was the roving gangs
of separatists that a pro-survivalist website he read warned him about.
His fervent reading of the website was also one of the reasons his wife
divorced him a year ago. That and Henry’s affection for semi-illegal,
but still a second amendment protected assortment of rifles and pistols.

“Those fucking ragheads ain’t coming into my neighborhood.” Henry spit
his words into the blue-blackness of his dark two-bedroom apartment.

He clamoured for his gun safe. Entered his personal lock code into the
electronic keypad - 2222.

He slipped a tactical vest around his shoulders. A pair of
semi-automatic pistols snapped into their holsters. Clips of ammo filled
the webbing of the tactical vest.

He selected his favorite AR-15. It was flat black with a rail accessory
system installed along the barrel. It allowed him to affix a special
sniper sight with night vision and forward pistol grip for easier
control. Tonight was the reason he acquired this arsenal. In his mind
the years of tireless preparation were finally vindicated.

“A well armed militia, being necessary to the security of a free state,
the right of the people to keep and bear arms, shall not be infringed,”
Henry muttered to himself as he slapped a 30-round clip into the
semi-automatic rifle.

He sucked his teeth and headed toward his front door, forgetting to put
on his work boots - they remained next to his bed in the blue light of
the bedroom. His bare feet tapped the tiled floor of his hallway as he
exited his apartment.

The Users were affixing small charges along the main electricity
transmission lines. The charges would destroy the cables and make repair
at least a three week-process. Just enough time for the fabric of
society to unravel just enough to gather political capital for the
powers that be to regain grip on the politics of the situation.

Henry’s breathing was labored. The weight of the vest heavy against his
shoulders. His pudgy belly strained the zipper of the tactical vest. The
cold of the concrete steps began to numb his toes.

It’s nights like these that the founding fathers envisioned. Henry
thought to himself. Unlikely, the Founding Fathers never envisioned a
semi-automatic rifle nor a 30-round magazine, but these particulars are
argument fodder for talking heads.

A flash and fizzle sparked from the substation down the alleyway.
Henry’s eyes refocused to the darkness. He could make out three figures.
There had to be more. These pigs don’t move without numbers. There are
more.

The mission objectives ticked off the heads up displays of the Users as
they affixed explosive charges to the assigned positions.

Movement. Twelve o’clock.

The perimeter User team trained their guns on a dark alleyway north of
their position. Switch to infrared.

The Users FILTERS switched their vision and blurbs of multi-colored red
and blue hues filled their eyes. A small yellow and red blob scooted
across the alleyway.

“Hold your fire.” One User commanded through their closed-link radio.
“Just a rat. Continue scanning. Mission not yet completed.”

The User’s held their fire and stood vigilant, like stone statues in the
darkness.

“I’ll just scare them off. Empty a clip and scurry around to the main
street,” Henry muttered to himself as he peered through his night vision
scope. His perch on the fire-escape would not allow for a quick escape -
he’d have to move to the alleyway then let loose.

“Movement. Not a rat this time,” a User whispered into his communication
link.

“Hold until confirmed hostile,” another User commanded.

“Sighting, crouched movement. Infrared hard to see weapon outline.
Movement suggests…”

“Confirmed, outline of weapon. Heat signature confirms heavily armed
hostile. Request clearance to fire.”

“Confirmed, fire when fired upon.”

The Users trained their weapons on the heat signature as it climbed down
from a fire-escape to the alleyway.

Henry clicked his safety off - his fingers shook from the adrenalin. He
did not notice his feet anymore nor that they had turned blue from the
cold and wet concrete.

Gunfire erupted from the alleyway. The Users returned fire - flooding
the alleyway with bullets. Henry did not finish emptying his clip as he
had planned.

The first bullet tore open his jugular vein. Blood sprayed in a wide
arch as Henry’s body twisted and rolled toward the middle of the
alleyway. His body caught numerous shots as it tumbled to the ground.

Positive hit. Cease fire. Cover the mission extract.

Users retreated from the substation - tapping each other on the shoulder
signaling their retreat back to the passenger van.

Henry’s finger clutched the trigger of his rifle - the click of the
firing mechanism echoed in the alleyway.

The Users were already secured in the van and speeding away from the
substation with its headlights off.

The gunfire awoke the remaining residents of the neighborhood. A number
of them were at their windows when the explosion triggered.

Glass shattered and struck the faces of sleep-filled eyes - cutting into
their flesh. Smoke and fire climbed skyward.

News channels erupted with reports of a substation exploding in the
small North Texas town of Wichita Falls. Soon other reports flooded into
newsrooms across the country about other substations exploding. Claims
of terrorism percolated into the news reports as blackouts rolled across
and covered the continental United States.

Fear and panic followed the darkness.

Robinson watched the news reports flood his secure-link from deep inside
Vision Systems command bunker.

“Operation successful.” A toothy grin spread across his face as he stood
behind the Operators at their terminals.

###

Trevor’s paranoia was not misplaced. He had been careful in his public
excursions. Just going to the market involved a snake-like pattern
through the cobblestoned streets of Prague.

He had been in the city for almost three weeks. Much of that time was
spent in a dark basement studio apartment rented from a man with one eye
and a thick Czech accent. Cash only. Paid three months in advance. It
bought Trevor privacy and anonymity. The Czech-man asked no questions
nor expected explanations. Prague was I. Fact the scene of much
espionage gameplay for many years between the U.S. and Russia.

The Czech Republic lay between two very difficult places for American
tactical teams or political powers to stretch their tentacles, but still
offered a liberal European refuge in comparison to Russian or Middle
Eastern enclaves. Anywhere else, Trevor would have likely stood out like
a sore thumb - it helped that he spoke a little Czech.

It was a calm cool day in the market. Trevor’s small kitchen pantry and
fridge were barren and his staple bottle of whiskey was three-quarters
dry. Thus he had to venture to the market. Bread. Cold-cuts. Cheese.
Alcohol.

The smiles were friendly as Trevor handed over the Czech Krona for the
small bag of groceries. The store owner Trevor knew. The nervous
stockboy was more nervous than usual. Something was not kosher on this
occasion.

Trevor heard the footsteps, but did not react in time. A short burst of
static in his implant combined with an electric shock to his ribs.
Blackness followed, with a headache following shortly thereafter.

Trevor awoke with a burlap sack over his head. His hands tied behind his
back to a wooden chair. He heard water dripping. Artificial light burned
through the burlap.

“This is rather clichē, guys,” Trevor could not resist sarcasm.

Footsteps approached. A shadow crossed between Trevor and the light
source. A hard right cross thundered into Trevor’s head. The chair
rocked to the side. Two hands steadied him before he could topple over.

“Nice, somebody was Golden Gloves,” Trevor felt his lower left bicuspid
wobbling in his jaw.

A chuckle sounded in the room.

“Take off the hood.”

As the hood ripped from his head, Trevor felt some of his hair go with
it.

Harsh white light blinded Trevor as the dizziness of the punch to the
head settled over him.

“You’ve got a few things to explain.” The voice was American with a
slight Southern drawl.

“Oh, Thank God! I was so worried they were gonna send some asshole with
a thick Russian accent.” Trevor spit saliva and blood on the floor. “I
can hardly understand those fucking people.”

“Listen, we’ve got limited time here, Mister...hmmm. It appears you
don’t have a last name.”

“Well, I had one once. But I didn’t like it. Just a few keystrokes to
get rid of it...at least in your file there,” Trevor said.

“Clever We don’t really need last names, do we?”

“We really don’t need first names either.” Trevor smiled a bloody grin.
“You’re going to have knuckles over there pound on me for a while. Then
you’re going to ask me the same questions while he takes a break. I’m
going to pull your chain and make comments about your dick size,
mothers’ dick size, and girlfriends’ dick size. You’ll smile smugly and
nod toward your punchy bitch over there. Then knuckles will come in for
a few more rounds. We can dance all night.”

“Very well, then. For the record, my name is Henderson and my girlfriend
has a very big dick. Give the man what he wants,'Knuckles', was it?”

The punches flew from both directions striking Trevor solidly with each
blow.

Trevor had activated his implant and his heads up display was fizzling
and flashing in and out with the barrage of punches. He did not know how
many punches to the head it would take to disable it. He guessed a few
more than would give him a detached retina or mild brain damage.
Uploading was the prime objective, finding time to get the upload
accomplished would trouble enough.

“Okay! Okay! Give me a minute!” Trevor pleaded.

“Wait, did Knuckles find a crack in your stoney sarcastic veneer?”
Henderson placed a cigarette in his lips and flicked his zippo open to
light it.

“No, I just wanted to…” Trevor coughed up some phlegm and blood. “I just
wanted tell you. Something important, before I forgot it..oh wait. Damn,
I swear I just had it!”

Henderson motioned for the punches to resume.

“Oh, wait, that’s it!” Trevor shouted before the punches could continue.
“You know those things are bad for you. You shouldn’t smoke.”

The barrage of punches continued. Knuckles had changed his pattern and
decided to include a couple of body shots, which allowed Trevor to drain
his mouth from the phlegm and blood pooling in it.

“OKAY! I’ll talk! No more bullshit! Henderson, call off Mike Tyson
here!”

The punches stopped.

Henderson inhaled deeply; the red ember glowed brightly at the end of
the cigarette.

“What do you want? I’m sure I’ve got something you want. Just keep
punchy on a fucking leash.” Trevor doubled over as his ribs resonated
with a harsh ache.

“The leaks. The files. Download them to this IP address,” Henderson laid
a slip of paper on Trevor’s knee. “Also, we want your two conspirators.
We know you’ve been communicating with them. It’s too random for us to
track. Get them to come out and play. Then we’ll scoop them up out of
the kitty litter.”

“If I don’t, it still doesn’t get you what you want. The files in my
head are encrypted. Even if you dug out my implant you wouldn’t get
shit.”

“Don’t go along and Knuckles, here, will be happy to test a theory of
mine on blunt force head surgery.”

A laugh escaped the boxer’s throat.

“I take it, the IP address that you are pinging the shit out of my brain
is where you want the files to go?” Trevor’s left eye had swollen shut.
He spit his bicuspid on the floor at the feet of the boxer.

“That should be the one. It’s the only signal in this room,” Henderson
leaned back and drew in a lung full of tobacco smoke.

“Well, I’m transmitting. Just give it a minute to start the download
process.”

They waited in silence. A tap sounded on the steel door. Henderson stood
up and went to the door. Exited.

“Knuckles?” Trevor asked. “Next time, you might want to start with the
body. Maybe a few strikes across the knees or legs. Going to the brain
just fucks up the whole thinking process.”

A few minutes later Henderson returned.

“Quit fucking playing your damn games, Trevor!”

“I got nothing. You wanted the shit I’m downloading it!”

“Your porn file isn’t what we wanted,” Henderson did not sit down.
“You’ve given us no other choice than to beat and hack it out of you!”

The Boxer stepped toward Trevor with fists at the ready.

“Meh, fuck it! Henderson? Knuckles? Eat a shit sandwich.”

A surge of electricity and smoke burst from Trevor’s right eye. The
Interrogators froze in horrified fear. Trevor activated a failsafe
protocol in his implant that fried the implant and released a deadly
amount of electricity throughout his nervous system.

He was dead in an instant.

The smoke and stench of death quickly filled the small steel room.

After a moment, Henderson checked for a pulse on Trevor, finding none.

“Did we get that on video?” Henderson called out. “Good, upload it to
the communication nets we know they’ve used before. That should spook
the rats out of the nest.”

Henderson reached for the door handle.

“What about him?” The Boxer asked.

“Bag him and tag him for cremation. He’s got nothing of value to us
anymore. The implant is burnt out. I’ve seen the protocol he used
before.”

23 - Lockdown

Consumed with rolling blackouts, the country’s dilapidated and neglected
infrastructure sputtered and stumbled to provide the power needed for
modern life in America. Rolling blackouts shuttered businesses and
crippled local economies. An effect is felt across the land, even on
Wall Street.

News channels advised people who do not need to keep electricity on for
medical purposes should limit their electricity use.

The President provided a speech from the Rose Garden at the Whitehouse,
but the dull roar of first-responder sirens in the background cut it
short. The last image was of the President being hurried through a
doorway by Secret Service Agents was not the comforting and reassuring
vision expected by the White House's Public Relations team.

Reports of looting following the setting of the sun began to spread as
wildfire across the internet.

Airports, trains stations, and highways were closed by Presidential
Order.

###

Reggie was too big a fish to stay in a fishbowl for long.

I’ve got no other choice, do I? Reggie sat isolated in a chair in the
waiting area of a gate inside the international terminal of the Toronto
Airport. I don’t think they’d even let me into the Duty-Free Shop right
now. His words typed into a screen on his heads-up display and whisked
off to the drafts folder of the email box Trevor, Jessica, and he used
to drop notes back and forth when they could not risk a chat room
session.

The ceiling to floor windows let all the light of the morning sun into
the terminal. The airspace around the airport prevented drones from
hovering outside the windows and training cameras on Reggie. A slight
reprieve from the unblinking eye of the 24-hour news cycle.

In addition to the blackouts, the Media whipped itself into a frenzy
focused on the lone hacker and disgraced former Congressional Chief of
Staff to the Late Representative Baker. The “fallen from grace”
storyline was beginning to get very tired. Reggie began to hate the
media that once praised him for having the courage to release the
information into the wild.

A number of “sightseers” had actually purchased a ticket on an
international flight to try and get a peek at Reggie as he wandered from
shop to shop in the international terminal of the airport. One was so
bold as to ask him for his autograph and a picture with him. Reporters
whose publications allowed deep expenses even courted him with promises
of first class tickets to any destination he wished for exclusive rights
to his story. Reggie declined and shunned their efforts. Reggie allowed
the courtship but made no promises. He made no plans and took no cash
promises or digital currency transfers. He refused the options of back
door exits into unmarked cars or vans, mainly because the CIA or some
other alphabet agency wanted him dead and they would not dare do it in a
place where he was basically on display. But Reggie grew tired of waving
to the cameras through the glass walls of the terminal and found one of
the secluded areas. It was only his visibility that kept him alive to
this point, so the fishbowl in the sunshine was his play for the time
being.

He was peppered with requests for interviews. So much so that he
completely shut down his secure-link only turning it on at the preset
times waiting for a ping from Trevor or Jessica. Withman was a ghost at
this point. He had too much data and his story backlogged was filled to
the brim. He did not need to clog it with a human interest piece about a
sad man stuck in limbo without a country or home.

He had survived on what little money he carried with him when he
wandered off the jet that brought him here. A late night flight from
Detroit.

When his money ran out, he found surprising friends and anonymous
supporters willing to front his expenses. He remembered it was a lonely
experience as he sat in a chair at one of the gates. That day he had not
eaten as his stomach growled and twisted as he watched the sunset over
the runways. Suddenly a to-go tray with a bottle of water and a small
sandwich was placed on the chair next to him. He noticed but ignored it,
assuming it was another traveler’s. He did not want to become the
world’s first international beggar-spy.

A voice said, “Please eat, my friend. It’s safe.”

His eyes flashed to the water and sandwich then upwards to a janitor
pushing a cleaning cart away from him.

He hesitated and scanned the terminal, looking for the clear wires
leading out of the backs of people’s ears thinking this was a sting
operation or the food was drugged.

He grabbed the food and unwrapped the sandwich, smelt it and tapped the
crust and meat of the sandwich to his tongue - testing to see if it had
a strange odor or flavor. It did not. And tuna fish never tasted so good
to him, until right then.

Had I really planned to ruin my life, I wouldn’t have picked Canada.
Nothing against Canada, it’s just too chummy with the powers that be in
the U.S.

The bottle of water followed the sandwich and the knowing glance from
the janitor followed a smile and small offerings from the terminal’s
support staff.

Emboldened by the good fortune, Reggie stepped to a counter to buy a
pack of chewing gum and a newspaper. He swiped his credit card to try
and pay and it was approved. The receipt read, You’re a hero to the
anonymous multitudes. Keep fighting. We’ll help where we can.

24 - Nothing more to give

Patricia Baker was never the political animal her husband was, but she
was not ignorant of the process and the tolls to be paid along the path
toward power. As she petted the framed photo of her and her children
with her late-husband digitally added to the background just to the side
of the tree behind them, she remembered the missed birthdays,
anniversaries, holidays, and funerals.

Now she found herself in the office of her late-husband. Housed in a
building she barely knew where the bathrooms were. In a city she did not
know. Surrounded by staffers whom she only recognized their voices when
calling this office. Right then, portions of the dark side of the moon
felt more at home for Acting-Florida Representative Patricia Baker.

Per the Florida State Constitution, the Governor announced she was to
hold her late-husbands office until the next election - a year from the
time of Rep. Baker’s untimely death.

“I lost the election for class secretary in law school! John won Class
President! He won everything!” Patricia Baker shouted into the phone
receiver. On the other end was the Governor’s aide relaying the news of
her appointment. “But, you don’t understand...I haven’t been to his
office in ages.”

She was not embellishing. She literally had not set foot in Rep. Baker’s
office in years. She came to Washington, with the objective of packing
up personal items, not doing her dead husband’s job.

The pause was pregnant. Patricia clasp her hand over her chest. The
responsibility suddenly began to squeeze her shoulders tight. She
suddenly understood why her husband’s hair had grown grey so quickly.
The Governor's aide rattled away at details on the reasons she was
holding this seat.

“The Governor wants this seat to remain with the party; you will hold
this position until relieved.”

The Governor’s past military experience never left him. He still barked
orders as if he never left his battleship - 20 years ago.

Patricia Baker hung-up the phone. She respectfully dropped into an
ornate sewing chair in her Florida home; her face aghast and cheeks
flushed. The late afternoon sunlight flooded the empty home. She brushed
some lint from her black suit jacket as tears dripped down her cheeks.
There was no pomp or circumstance. No swearing-in ceremony. No hand on
the Bible. No flash of cameras.

With a shouted order and flick of pen, Patricia Baker was the newest
member of the United States Congress and she was alone in the husk of
the world she shared with her late-husband.

Time waits for no one. Instantly, Patricia Baker’s handheld device
chirped with a messages, texts, and emails. She hated these things more
than her late-husband once despised. It was pressed into her hand
shortly after her security detail arrived and informed her that Reggie
Watts - her late-husbands long-time Chief-of-Staff - had disappeared and
rumors and reports began to flood the news cycle about a high-tech
security firm with government contracts actually spying on AMerican
citizens.

The leaks caused all staff on the hill to deactivate their secure-links.
Blackberry’s were reactivated and suddenly phone chargers were the new
must-have item on Amazon.com.

The Blackberry chirped again. Then it rattled on the wooden desk. Once
again, this was the prized possession of the public-service elite.
Presidents refused to give them up when elected. The QWERTY keyboard
suffered at the madthumbs of frantic staffers and Congressmen. Replaced
by touchscreens, then wearable technology, and ultimately internal
implants, the Blackberry went the way of the Dodo bird.

No longer and now resurrected from the bottom drawers and dusty file
boxes of the bowels of Capitol Hill. You see, the old hardware still had
something that was prized in an age of high-tech spying and subterfuge -
encryption. Encryption that was manageable and scalable based on the
individual handset and the user. Sadly, when technology evolved into the
brains of the users, this element was left behind. People got used to
having a biometric reader sync with their DNA or retina patterns and
even the most astute user forgot the numbers and strings of hexadecimal
code that required the user to affirmatively engage with technology. In
short, everything the user understood was too passive. Passive lent
itself to manipulation. Manipulation yielded to brute force. Brute force
ultimately conquered.

Patricia swept the Blackberry up in her hand and tapped the keyboard.
The screen lit up and digital image of her and her late-husband greeted
her with a prompt to tap her passcode.

She tapped the number for her wedding anniversary.

No good.

She tapped her daughter's birthday.

Incorrect.

Then she remembered the correct code.

112263.

She and her late-husband held a deep and often obsessive interest in
John Fitzgerald Kennedy. Especially, the date of his assassination.

A message flashed on her screen wiping away the previous image. It was a
download request from the Security Administrator.

Patricia sucked her teeth, “stupid updates.”

She accepted the download and tossed the handheld back onto the wooden
desk.

Her gaze traversed the former office of her husband. She cursed him for
leaving her. She wanted him to retire years ago, but he refused. He
loved his work. He loved politics and public service. She trained her
eyes on his bookshelf, then a portrait of Dolly Madison.

John Baker held a special appreciation for First Lady Dolly Madison.

“Without her,” He said. “We wouldn’t have any of the archives.”

This led to an extended description of the War of 1812 and when the
British burned Washington. John admired the grit it took for Dolly
Madison to grab what she could carry and escape. What she so happened to
grab was the Constitution and Bill of Rights among other important
documents and artifacts.

“We wouldn’t have our country, but for Dolly!”

Patricia could hear her husband’s words echoing in her mind.

The Blackberry buzzed violently on the desk.

“This Goddamn thing…” Patricia exclaimed as she swiped it up into her
hand. “I ought to…”

She stopped before throwing it across the office and saw the message on
the screen.

Urgent. Read. John was murdered.

Her heart skipped. The message was signed, 112263.

There was only one other person that knew that code.

“Reggie?” Patricia tapped the message and a file labeled “Baker” opened
and a report populated the screen. The sync completed and brief text
message populated the screen.

You’re not going to believe it, but it’s all true. I’ve uncovered more
than the world needs to know. We have to see it to the light of day.
John would have wanted that. The news barely scratches the surface. We
have to get it on the Congressional Record. Into hearing notes. Warren
commission level. Senate Joint Committee on Investigations getting data
dump now. Need you to work the inside, tenderize the ribs. John had file
son all his “friends” in the Senate. See locked file drawer left side of
the cabinet behind the desk. If you get push back, use the file on the
bastards and burn asses to the ground. Leak outside if needed, but the
best context will come from within. I wish I could be there with you,
but I’m a dead-man if I set foot on the Hill. You have to do it on your
own.

Patricia’s eye began to well with tears. Reggie was her dead husband’s
most trusted aide. She knew of his drinking problem from John. They
talked closely about Reggie because they treated him as their own son.
The joke was that Reggie’s skin tone was a product of the strong Florida
sun. All that washed away now, but the connection remained as strong.

“Oh Reggie,” She gasped and cradled the phone in her hands.

Doubts crept into her mind about if she could do what her dead husband
had just tasked her with. It was like a small piercing pain in a place
you don't remember having a pain before but suddenly you feel it and
remember so much about yourself.

“I am to hold this position until relieved,” Patricia wiped her eyes and
gritted “her teeth. “James! Get me Senator Harris from the Senate
Committee on Investigations!”

25 - “An Intentional Act”

The flight disappeared from radar and remained unresponsive despite
repeated contact attempts by flight controllers.

One hundred forty-eight passengers and crew perished when Flight 114
crashed into a remote portion of the Austrian Alps just a few hundred
kilometers from the small ski town of Innsbruck. Mechanical failures
were instantly suspected by all the news outlets reporting from wire
reports and sources at the National Transportation Safety Board.

It was not until two days after the crash that the news broke about the
pilot intentionally crashing the plane.

“This was an intentional act,” the translator spoke over the German
explanation of the Chief Prosecutor leading the investigation. “I was
given this information just a short time ago. And from the recordings
reclaimed from the recovered black-boxes we can say the pilot was not in
the cockpit and only the Co-Pilot was at the helm.”

The flutter of camera shutters and flashbulbs arose like the laugh-track
of a cheaply produced situational comedy as the Chief Prosecutor paused
form reciting the prepared statement.

“The Co-Pilot was a young professional that had roughly one thousand
hours of flight time on this particular plane and from our on-going
investigation has no obvious ties to radical groups or a history of
violent or destructive behavior.”

The Chief Prosecutor began to describe the series of events he
determined from the vague recordings. He said the Captain left the
cockpit to use the restroom in the forward cabin and after found himself
locked out of the cockpit. After his commands to unlock the cockpit door
when unheeded, the Captain began to attempt to break down the cockpit
door. The Co-Pilot never responded to the Captain’s commands but,
according to the Chief Prosecutor, remained calm and breathed normally
as he programed the flight computer to begin a descent that ultimately
led the aircraft to crash into the Austrian Alps.

###

Gustaf Stuber was a 31 year-old pilot for a small airline operating out
of Dusseldorf, Germany. He was a former German military pilot who served
in a number of support missions in the recent civil war in Afghanistan.
Those missions were a part of a United Nations peacekeeping mission in
the region trying to keep the country from being chopped up and annexed
by its surrounding neighbors, again after nearly a thousand years of
constant war, civil and otherwise.

Stuber served honorably and received high accolades from his commanding
officers prior to his early retirement after he suffered injuries from a
hard landing after a landing gear malfunctioned on his jet. Part of him
missed flying military jets, but his new position gave him a small
reminder of the joy of flying for his country. He approached the work
with the same dedication and respect. He never doubted he would fly jets
until even the civilian world prohibited it.

He lived a quiet life on a modest pension from the German Government and
recently began training with the airline to seek higher ratings on
larger aircraft with the hopes of qualifying for longer flights and a
higher salary. He was unmarried and lived in a small apartment near
Dusseldorf, Germany. He loved only flying and held no other mistress,
aside from football scores of course.

Gustaf Stuber was the co-pilot behind the controls of the flight that
crashed outside of Innsbruck, Austria. He was not a pedophile nor a
radical as it would later be suspected in the news media then confirmed
via a cache of incriminating evidence discovered on his personal
computer. He was a convenient patsy for a larger conspiracy to prevent
the spread of extremely sensitive information that would have pulled
back the curtain on a widespread network of international espionage and
unlawful government surveillance.

On Stuber’s laptop, in addition to an incriminating cache of horrible
images involving underage children engaged in abhorrent sex acts, was a
flight simulator program that he used to practice his flight skills. On
that program there was a saved flight simulation that drew an eerie
coincidence to the his last flight. The metadata on the program
indications that Stuber practiced this flight simulation a number of
times just days before his untimely death. Stuber did in fact use this
program, however he had not logged any hours on it since picking a
regular commuter route that recently only allowed him a day off a week.
Close inspection of the system information would indicate the times
logged for the simulation were impossible because Stuber was over a
thousand miles away during much of the simulator's testimony. This fact
was oblivious to the investigation after the crash as insurance
companies, airlines, and governments wanted a quick conclusion to
reassure the public Stuber was a “rogue madman” and air travel was again
safe for all.

Unbeknownst to Stuber this program was not a genuine flight simulator.
He had used the program for years. But a recent software patch installed
carried with it a subroutine that had been subconsciously programing him
to do exactly what he had done in causing the intentional crash of
Flight 114.

The software reported back through its algorithm when a user trained and
how they trained. The subroutine then implanted a hypnotic scheme into
the user's subconscious that upon trigger would make the user believe
they were experiencing a most ideal flight, despite the reality
surrounding them. This was in fact the case and this particular
application plied its effects on Stuber.

When a user was needed for a mission the hypnotic subroutine would run
and implant the experience to play during the most opportune time or
when the pilot flew next.

This subroutine was not specifically intended for Stuber, but rather it
had been subconsciously programing every person that used the program
since it was downloaded; essentially creating an arsenal of hypnotized
pilots at OpOne’s disposal. Stuber was the right pilot using the right
software for the right flight - the next bullet to go in the chamber. A
simple algorithm allowed OpOne to fine tune his last session the night
before he was scheduled to fly this particular flight; the flight that
was scheduled to be the setting of an exchange of very important data.

The violent end reported by the news media was never intended by Stuber.
His mind believed he was flying the designated flight plan and nothing
was wrong. He was not coerced or threatened; he was simply the patsy.

His mind did not hear the demands from his Captain. He did not realize
the trajectory he programed into the flight computer would send all
occupants of Flight 114 to their deaths on the side of an Austrian
mountain. He never intended to crash the plane. This is the only
explanation for why he did not respond to his Captain and why he
breathing was calm and relaxed on the flight recording. To his eyes, he
was calming following the pre-designated flight plan he and the Captain
programed into the flight computer.

The Captain’s knuckles were bloodied and cut from punching the
reinforced door to the cockpit.

Stuber breathed normally.

The stewards and stewardesses were holding each other with tears
streaking their faces.

Stuber grinned at the perceived horizon of blue sky against the craggy
Alps. He thought to himself, this is some of my best flying. It’s almost
like a simulator, but so real.

The passengers shrieked and cried as they saw the Captain battle with
the immovable door.

Stuber checked the altitude and wind speed, as he had trained, and noted
all optimal readings. He thought, my simulator back home was so
realistic that each flight felt like the one before. Amazing.

Prayers erupted from the throats and minds of faithful and atheists
alike as the the mountains grew larger in their porthole windows.

The frenzied panic boiled over as the tips of the wings cut through
treetops and found the bite of the rocks and soil. The plane struck and
then disappeared into a fiery explosions and cloud of flame and smoke
against a broad cliffside of a mountain.

Gustaf Stuber was now very much the villain of countless news reports
with his name hissed by all who relayed the reports.

###

“I fail to see how this was a proper use of our resources.” OpOne
mumbled to himself.

Robinson grunted in the cool darkness of his study. A half empty bottle
of scotch inches from a thick glass with a thin layer of brown liquid
with Robinson’s black and creased fingers wrapped around it.

“Is the cover story taking root?” Robinson raised the glass to his lips
as he spoke aloud, seemingly to himself but really via the secure link
between he and OpOne.

“Yes, the new outlets are reporting that this guy was just an ordinary
pilot. Under experienced, but not a radical.” OpOne replied.

“What about the planted medical history we spearfished into his company
file?” Robinson rested his head against the cushion of his desk chair
with his eyes closed.

“It’s there, and likely to come out either today or within the next news
cycle. Once the local authorities get into his apartment, they’ll find
his laptop and the cache of child pornography we planted. He’ll be
demonized within a few hours.”

“Good,” Robinson dragged his fingers across the etched design of the
glass in his hand. “Are there reports on the passenger manifests, yet?”

His secure-link pinged with links to articles published in The Wall
Street Journal and New York Times. One article reported passengers
included residents of the United States, England, Canada, and Iran, but
mainly the passengers were of German residency.

“Good,” Robinson wiped the links away from his heads-up display with a
swipe of his hand.

“They’ll never know it was us.” OpOne muttered to Robinson. “Doubtful
even that the papers will ever connect the dots on the backgrounds of
the Americans, Brit, and Iranian on the plane. It’s being reported the
American was a contractor for an accounting firm in Boston. Nothing
interesting in that, so I doubt a reporter will pickup on the connection
to the audit his firm was conducting of our overseas operation. So that
cover story is likely to hold.”

“Then we stopped the exchange from happening?” Robinson asked.

“In the most extreme sense of the word,” OpOne answered confidently.
“They may have traded the data between themselves on the plane, per
their plan. Might have even been reviewing on a device, but that flight
didn’t have wi-fi for the passengers and I found no transmissions from
the plane or the IP addresses we had traced and connected to the
suspects.”

“Good, no damage.” Robinson affirmed.

“Yeah, if 148 people dead isn’t considered damage.” OpOne growled.

“Collateral...nothing more.” Robinson set his glass on his desk and
braced his hands on the arms of his chair and stood up. “Keep me posted
if anything develops.”

Robinson clicked off from the link before OpOne could answer
affirmatively.

26 - Blacked Out

The Third Street Tunnel runs under the National Mall of Washington, D.C.
In this tunnel a white panel van sputtered to halt; steam rose from
under its hood. The driver did not panic or exhibit concern. His latex
gloved hands slipped the transmission into park and tapped the hazard
light switch. Yellow lights rhythmically flashed illuminating the dark
tunnel as cars and trucks barreled through the tunnel past the
distressed van.

Just a couple hundred feet above the tunnel through concrete, tunnels,
and cables stood the U.S. Capitol with the office buildings of both
houses of Congress to either side. Tourists and commuters passed through
crosswalks as cars idled behind red lights.

In the office buildings, Congressional aides and staffers emailed and
chatted on phones - cellular and voice-over-internet-protocol. The
